COUNT y BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON.- PARLIAMINTARY ELECTION, 1906. TO THE ELECTOR'S.

OFMTLVMEN, Mr. Balfour has at length been compelled, by hopeless disorder within his own party. to take the course which Public opinion had long demanded in vain. He has resigned. and a Liberal Government representative of all that is most vigorous and distinguished in the progres- swisehnlovententi of our time now seeks your confi.lance it I Programme which it has frankly placed before the country. Honoured as lamby an invitation from the Liberal Association and Progressive Labour League of Warring- ton, it if alike my privilege and duty to fight for the cause of Progress to the best of my ability in our town. The issues which depend mum the decision of the electorate are of the GRAVEST IMPORTANCE. Behind us lie ten years of rf -action, unnecessary wars and rumours of ware, extravagance, and what John Bright all his days denouncad—Tory class legislation. TA return the Conservative party again to power would be to repeat all these disasters and to enorm.ousl v aggravate them by a revival of the hateful system of Protection. The alternative le a Liberal policy of peaCer conoillation. economy end genuine social reform for the benefit and advancement of the people. The mandate given to the late government in the General Election of 1900 on behalf of their Imperial Polley abroad, has been exploited by them to serve the interests of the privileged classes and vested Interests at home. They have yielded to the most selfish demands of monopoly and mita), and of the party of ascendency in the North of Ireland, increased the burel•ns of the poor and fettered the freedom of labour. Indolent in legislation, yet activa in creating Compli- cations.they have as a climax to their miscloinga in South Africa introdneed an odious form of Chinese Labour, while et home they have unsettled commerce by a pro- Pont to return to return to Protection, and to a tax on the people's food, and have thro en Our system of Educa- tion into a turmoil of confusion and strife. We most fight once more the old battle for LIBERTY OF coNSCIRNOB, for the RIGHT OF PUBLIC CONTROL OVER PUBLIC EXPENDITURE, and the LIEVRATION OF THE NEOESSARIES OF LIFE FROM BURDRNSOMB TAXATIoN. I STAND AS A FREE TRADER, and I will give my STRONGEST OPPOSITION, not only to Mr. Chamberlain's proposals for food taxes and a general tariff, but also to Mr. Balfour's ill-defined policy of retaliation. My views on the problem of Irish Governmert which is ever present with us. are well known, and I stated them clearly in my letter to the papers about a fortnight since. I hold that in Ireland.as in England, Government should be OF the people. FOR the people, and BY the people, awl that we must constantly work towards that ideal—R solution beneficial from every point of view In Ireland, end making for peace and the increased strength and permanence of our great empire. CH MESE LABOUR. I feel most etrongiv that the introduction of Chinese Labour into South Africa is an UTTBU ABOMINATION. Lord Selborne In his latest memorandum admits that many of the Chinese did not understand the terms of their agreements, and it is NO EXAGGERATION TO BAY that the coalitions under which these Chinese are - -4 are a SPECIES OF SLAVERY. netters freoly admit they adopted this policy `, Trades Unionism ; and the proportion ot labour . ri2md as compared with coloured much more Hum br. cent. and In many cases I hold that in justice , what it was formerly. workers and In the future interests of the . a SINISTER and GitADlNti system should be • led as speedily as possible. My strong ooinlons in regard too the TAXATION OF LAND VALUItS, and the amendment of JUDGE-Hum . LAW AS IT AFFbCTS THADES U 171055, are I be- lieve well known in Warrington. Die mainly on the issues referred to in this address that I venture to appeal to you for support. Should you do me the honour to elect me as your representative in Parliament Ishall strive to do all I can to further the local interests of our town, and the general well-being of

I

I am, gentlemen, 1 War faithful friend. ?atten Arms Hotel. ARTHUR H. CROSFIRLD. Warrington. Jima. sth, 1906. . - ------- ---- _ LONDON AND NORTH-WESTERN RAILWAY. WHITE ROSE.

b, GERALD BIS&

CHAPTER XXXVI.

!I EACH for himself," muttered lan Frazar, as he drove to Holborn Viaduct. "This damned show has burst up a bit too soon, or I should have feathered my nest out of it a bit better. As it is, I've only got his Grace's monkey; but I may be able to raise a bit more in Bavarienburg. I only wish that I could have persuaded that sublime ass of a Rudolph" (here his lip curled contemptuously) " to bolt-too. I could have lived comfortably on him for the rest of my life, and had some sort of a locus slandi. But the idiot insisted on riding the high horse. Damn 'him, kick- ing me out like that I I only wish I had had time to stop and give him a hiding : but it wasn't worth it. Anyhow, it won't be long before he pays for it; and I shall get a cheap revenge." On the platform with a start he recognised Ellis Gordon's back, as the latter entered a carriage. So he took the utmost precaution to avoid him both in the train and on the boat. "He'd want to go halves," he said to him- self, as he sat back in his seat, meditatively puffing at one of the duke's best cigars. "It will be time to make terms if I find that he, too, is bound for l3ayarienburg." However, to his relief he saw nothing of him after Cologne: and he proceeded on his jour• ney with a lighter heart. It was late the following night before he reached Bavarienburg ; and he put up at the principal (hotel, intending early the following morning, to seek an audience with the King. f The latter, he knew, would have very scant sympathy with his son's indiscretion, as the Stuart blood was on the side of his late wife and had nothinc, to do with himself. More- over, he would be afraid lest he might find himself seriously compromised with Great Britain, and, consequently, in disfavour with the Kaiser. lan Frazer laid out a rough, plan of cam- paign over his last cigar, deciding, however, to allow affairs to shape themselves finally at the interview, when be had had a chance of seeing how the King took it. So he went to bed with high hopes that the morrow might render the "White Rose" sclieme not quite so abortive so far as his own ends were ,:oneerned. .

At ten o'clock h? lyAdle approached the castle and was immediately recognised as the strange Englishman who had a few days before come to see Prince Rudolph, so he had little difficulty in obtaining an audience .with one of the officials of the household. _

To him he refused to reveal one syllable of his business.

" Tell his Majesty that Mr. lan Frazer, of the Diplomatic Service, begs for a private audience with a view to communicating some- thing of the highest importance with regard to Prince Rudolph." " Prince Rudolph ? " exclaimed the official with a start of surprise. " Yes, it is, a very private matter," pursued Frazer coolly ; "and I should esteem it a great favour if you would be so kind as to deliver my message at once, and bring me his Majesty's answer. Iliad the honour of a personal inter- view with the Prince himself only two days ago."'

With a puzzlzd look on his face the other bowed and withdrew.

"I will .see what can be done in the matter," he said enigmati‘...ally. Five minutes lat.:!r a man of thirty entered the room im.petuouslv.

" You to se ;:i the .N.in4 ? " he asked abruptly. - Frazer blandly signinocl a.sgont. a With reference to Prince Rudolph's affairs? " Again Frazer bowed.

"Are you not the Englishman who came over to see Prince Rudolph a fzw days ago..?:' " I am," replied Frazer, " and I haa the honour of a long interview with " Ah," said the other, for the first :time fully realising the situation. " And what, pray," he added suavely, "i thii i'-uportant. private conuirazdeatioa of yours about Prince Rudolph ? " . .

That, sir," said Frazer, smili matically, " i 5 between the King an,l - :t.' • " Really ? " said the other. " • intimate with the Prince? "

" I have that honour," replied Fratf.T. • " have seen a great deal of him parsonall•,- dur- ing the last few days and iiv, in fact, 'become, very intimate with him." '. . • . ". Oh, awl is it fr9m him that you • bring - message ? " - •,.

• " Nat ctly, but lam in his confidence ; and there is a very private matter which cannot be trusted to paper which it would interest his Majesty to learn of," . .

" It is very kind of you, Mr. Frazer," said the other coldly, 'to take all this trouble on Prince Rudolph's behalf."

" Not at all," said the Scotsman depreca- tingly "it is an honour and a pleasure to do a service to such a generous patron as the King of Bavarienburg is reported to be" "Oh," said the other in an incisive tone, "It's a question of blackmail, is it ? 7 " Sir, exclaimed Frazer, starting forward with simulated indignation, "how dare you? You shall account to me for this."

"Hoity-toity, my good fellow; how dare /, Indeed ? " rejoined the young Bavarien- burger, drawing himself up to his full height and growing warm with the discussion. "Do you know who I am ? " Frazer shook his head negatively with a disparaging smile. "I am Prince Rudolph of Bavarienburg," roared the other in, a passion; "so now you know me."

" Prince Rudolph ? " exclaimed Frazer puzzled. "Why, I left him in London thirty-six hours ago !" " When he kickcd you downstairs," con- cluded the real prince 'grimly. Silence fell on both; and Frazer grew livid up to the eyes. "Yes, my blackmailing friend," went on Rudolph, regaining his self-control with an effort, "1 am Prince Rudolph. He who has personated me in London with your damnable White Rose' committee is Count Otto von Adelstein, my foster brother and clearest friend—ambitious for me. I refused to have anything to do with your original proposal; and he—my poor, dear Otto—acted foolishly, wrongly, rashly, quixotically behind my back, hoping to further my interests without my knowledge. Here," he went on, producing a bundle of closely written pages, "is his con- fession, written on the eve of his death, and entrusted to his valet, who arrived at mid-- night last night. And now he is dead," he went on, burying Isis face in his hands, "he is dead—my own beloved Otto. He died for his Rudy's sake; he sinned for his Rudy's sake; and now I have to send these, his last dear words to me, to the King of England, the Emperor of Germany, and strangers, to exonerate myself. My heart is broken at the loss of my ptto "; and for a moment the strong man gav€ way, shaking with sob. For the first time in his life a great fear and a great shame seized Frazer : and he wished that the earth would swallow him up. Suddenly, with an effort the Prince pulled himself together and approached him with blazing eyes. "And then—and then, you, you dirty mean, cowardly hound, you who for your own ends led him, on to his death, come here to blackmail one who would have been a broken- hearted father had things been as you fondly imagined! you—vou cur ! " The Prince raised his hand as though tc strike the cowering man, who failed to keep up his usual bold front and cool assurance under the lash of the other's tongue. But Rudolph checked himself.

_ " I have too much pride to touch you," he said with an infinite scorn in his coldest voice, pointing to the door. " Go, lest I forget myself; and may God have more mercy upon your pitiful soul than you have shown to others ! "

And lan Frazer slunk out of the room abashed for once—an exile and a broken man.

CHAPTER XXXVH.

THREE months later Ben and Bina were sitting in the twilight in the drawing-room of Lady Grizelle's cosy country-house. The girl was but a shadow of her former self. The whiteness of her thin face ac- centuated her dark eyes, and her short curly hair gave her a strange piquant appearance. She had lost all her old hauteur of manner, and there was a grave expression in her eyes which was new. She had had a terrible time and the doctors marvelled that she had lived through it. The attack of brain fever had been vcry severe and protracted, as she had insisted upon being told the truth as soon as consciousness returned and the shock had thrown her right back again. Since then she had not spoken on the subject. Her whole soul and life appeared to be bound up in Ben, and she shrank from the outer world.

Ben Nevis himself appeared to be as strong and well as ever, though he had partially lost his bronzed appearance. It was early in May, the month for wed. dings and both her hands were confidingly mtrusted to his care.

"At last," he said softly, "to-morrow darling, you will be altogether mine; and I wil: take you right away from everybody, and we'll travel and travel all over the uorld, wherever the sun is, avoiding winter and searching for happiness." AN EXPENSIVE JOURNEY.

At Warrington on Monday, Robert Porter. of no settled abode,was cliarged with travelling on the London and North-Western Railway from Widnes to Warring- ton at a quarter past eleven on Saturday night without having paid his fare. Tt wpm stated that the prisoner arrived at the low level station shortly after eleven o'clock. Ile got out of the compartment and looked about the platform to see if there were any means of getting away. He made a rush for the exit and tried to pass the ticket collector. He was stopped and when asked for his ticket said he had not come by train and healso refused to pay his fare. He again attempted to pass the ticket collector and also pushed him about.— Prisoner said he went on the station to get a parcel and denied baying cone by train.— A fine of 10s. and costs was imposed, or, in default of payment, fourteen days' imprisonment,

TFS THOUSAND AT TEA.—On Tuesday evening the New Year tea parties in connection with the Sunday Schools of Blackburn churches and chapels concluded The custom of tea drinking on the first day of every year is one of long standing, and upwards of ten thou- sand children are estimated to have taken part in this years celebration, in addition to many elder scholars.

"The man who grasps that door-handle will shake hand 8 with certain death."

The speaker was Mr. McGill, my late husband's partner, and as he stood looking at me out of his grey, thoughtful eyes he gave point to his strange speech by laying his finger on the door in question. I am anything but a brace woman, and I shud- dered a little at his words, though it was my defence that he was planning. He saw this, and a very reassuring smile broke out all over his rather rugged face, lighting it up strangely. "Ye don't need to be frightened any more, Frs. Hammond," he said. "And I'm only sorry that ye didn't ask me to come down here a week or two earlier when these thieves first began their work, instead of suffering the fright I can see has been pulling ye down." "It has been a fearful time for me. I have endured agonies every night, fearing they would come here. The place stands all alone. There's no one in it except the children. my two scared maids, and myself; and there's plenty for the thieves to get, if they were to break in." . .

We' had hd a series of burglaries in Walsington that had thrown the whole neighbourhood into a panic, and the thieves had shewn themselves so skilful and daring that no one could trace them, and they were so accurately informed as to where it was worth their while to go that everyone who had anything to lose was scared. "Well, they may break in here, even when I've done what I mean to do.; but if they break out again, they're cleverer than I think them, and I know nothing of my business or of electricity." He spoke with a dry deliberation that was all suggestive of absolute confidence. cI had been completely unnerved by the bur- glaries, and the strain of the nights of sleepless terror I had passed through had so broken me down that I had sent for Mr. McGill to come and advise me what to do. soon worKeu my-se:f rmu a stat. en: suen nervous dread that I found myself listening acutely for sounds in the lower part of the house ; and as everyone knows who has ever lived in an old house there are enough sounds to be heard to frighten any listener. I was doing some needlework that night, and I was so irritable and nervous that even the rustling of the stuff I was working worried me. More than once I stopped to listen as some fitful gust of wind 'found its way down the chimney, or rattled a door, or moaned or sighed somewhere in the house.

I could not get the recollection of the curate's words out a my thoughts, and I wished that I had sent my valuables away before. 4‘ Think what might happen before any help could reach you." These words kept recurring to me, and each time With a more vivid suggestion of possible Violence. As I glanced across to where soy darlings .lay asleep, cuddled in one another's arms, I, positively hun- gered for the morning to come. Twice I opened my door in the belief that I could hear someone moving in the house, and only after straining to listen did I convince myself that My fears were groundless. I am such a 'coward that it took much effort before I could shake off the feeling of impending danger sufficiently to put out the light and slip into bed. Even then I could not sleep. A momentary doze only brought me a terrible dream in which my thoughts took a fearsome shape, and 'awoke shiver- ing and more frightened than before. As I. lay thus trenibiing in my fear I-did indeed hear a noise that set ever) nerve in my body tingling with cramping cold. A distinct sound of breakim• glass, which seemed to come from the room und•-rnenth mine—the dining-room. In a mummy I was out of Oed lying at full length listening with my ear pressed close to the floor. Someoot was moving about in the ...own halaw. What should Ido •My heart was beating with great lurching thnnips ' against my ribs, and th: wildest thoughts rushed into my head. The horre... of my dreams combined with my terror at what I heard, seemed to paralyse me completely. I could think of nothing con- nectedly. My one idea was that my children were at the mercy of the villains who had broken into the house.

For the moment I forgot all about the protection that hedged me around; all about the alarm bell that I could set clanging with a touch to rouse the whole countryside. Everything was swallowed up in the thought that my children were in deadly peril. The one recollection that came to me was of violence of the thieves in some of the other robberies.

How long I stayed in this half-torpid condition of abject fright I cannot tell. To me it seemed a lifetime. The agony of a coward's fear is not , measured by minutes and hours. I passed through a storm of wild, unreasoning terror as I lay with my head pressed close to the floor and held my breath in my desperate eagerness to hear the move- ments of the thieves. After the first noise I could hear nothincf. All was once more perfectly silent, and a faint stir of hope moved in me that, after all, I had been mis- taken.

But the next instant dissolved my doubts..

A fearful cry, wild, shrill, and unearthly, rang through the house, and at the same moment the sound of a heavily falling body came up to me from the room below.

The scream set loose my terror-tied wits. I recognised the cause instantly. I rushed to the alarm-bell and set it clanging and clashing and echoing far and wide on the still night air, and at the same moment the two servants knocked loudly st my door, and in voices all discordant with fear cried out to know what had happened. I had scarcely switched off the current and admitted them before the neighbours arrived and bells were ringing and blows on the front door were sounding furiously. Leaving one of the maids with the children I ran down with the other to let in the neighbours. Then in a body we moved into the dining-room where I had heard the noise. As soon as the door was opened, and this was only done with difficulty on account of some obstruction, there was a loud outcry. The body of a man lay on the floor twisted into a horrible contortion as though in the writhing agony of a fearful and violent death. The head was face downwards, and when one of the men turned the head and looked at the features there was another loud cry of astounded surprise. The •burglar was the Rev. Thomas Wrathall himself!

Mr. McGill was hugely delighted at the success of the new burglar trap, and he declared that it would make his fortune. I sent for him, of course, as soon as the truth was known, and be explained to everybody at great length and with proportionate satisfaction all that he had done. More than that, he brought down with him a certain police detective who recognised the curate as one of the most skilful and original thieves of the day. Then things were clear enough. The man had personated a clergyman with a spotless reputation of the name of Wrathall, and. having first burgled Dene Court successfully, had himself organised the means designed for his own detection, and had then used his opportunites to find out where there was anything worth stealing, taking care, of course, that the amateur patrols should be far enonali away from any particular house which he robbed. "That last visit, Mrs. Hammond," said Mr. McGill, "was simply to find out that ye had not yet sent your jewels out of the house, while his urgency that ye should send them away on the following day was just a blind to increase the improbability of his being regarded as the thief. Indeed, if he hadn't been caught as he was I don't see how ye'd ever have found him out at all. Ye've had a very lucky escape," and he accompanied the words with a very significant smile, "and maybe now ye'll see your inability to protect yourself and the bairns, and just give me the right to look after ye all."

I agreed with this very gladly ; but there have been no more burglaries in Walsington. [THE END.]

This it was that frightened me into sending for Mr. McGill. When he came in answer to my letter, he listened very intently to all I had to say, asked a few ques- tions, looked carefully round the house, then went all over it, and at the close of his examination was in a state of great but calm satisfaction, when telling me his plan. "The chance is just what I've wanted for a long time, and inside of three days I'll make the place just as impregnable as a fortress in one respect, and quite as safe-holding as a gaol in another. But now, mark," and he spoke very earnestly, "ye'll need to do exactly what I tell ye. Above all, not a word or syllable to any soul on earth." "I shall tell no one," I answered, readily; "except, of course, the curate." He looked across at me and the grey eyes shone from under his rather shaggy brows with a light that reminded inc somewhat of a certain interview we two had once bad—before I was married. He had been a very old friend of mine even before he had become a friend of my husband. "And who may the curate be ? " he asked. The answer involved a rather long explanation. The curate was the Rev. Thomas Wrathall, who had been unexpectedly put in charge of the parish when the vicar had been packed off to the south of France for the winter. WOMEN'S CHAT

A New Year Superstition.—As a rule women play the principal part in ghost stories, family legends, and superstitions, but an exception is afforded by the old superstition, that it is lucky to encounter a dark man the first thing in the New Year, end unlucky to meet a fair man. This appears at first to be contrary to the general imagination, which rather pictures a fair man, and not a dark one, in thinking of anyone of benign nature and countenance. The drama for instance, in- variably presents the villian of the piece as a dark visaged person. Why it should be considered unlucky to meet a fair man is therefore not very clear, but the superstition, like so many others of its kind, is supposed tohave' originated from the sctiptnres. The belief that 3ndas Iscariot was red-haired, no doubt , gave rise to this curious New Year tradition, Art the same as the ill luck of thirteen at table, may be traced to the fact of that being the number who partook of the Last Sunper. A Spanish Royal Marriage.—The Ring of Spain is understood to be fell of plans and preparations for his Own wedding, and that event • somewhat overshows the forthcoming marriage of his only, surviving sister, the Infanta Maria Teresa,who is to marry her cousin Prince Perdinard of Bavaria. The Ring's elder sister, the late Princess of the Asturias, died some fifteen months ago, • and it is her little son and daughter, who, for the time being, stand next in order to Ring Alfonso in succes- sion to the throne. The marriage of the late Princess of the Afstnriaa with Do a Carlos of Bourbon, was the subject of a fierce political agitation, in Spain, but the fortheotning wedding of the Ring's other sister is ! more favourably regarded by the Spanish people. LI. We are very previous ; when February arrives. spring headgear is the order of the day, and we are ashamed to take our walks aboard beneath felt and beaver. The new straws for early spring wear, will be shewn as soon as the sales are over, and those among us who can afford few changes, will do well to push along with our winter stock *ring the present month, and not be tempted to buy up cheap hats or toques that must so soon be laid aside. The new models are medium in size, and somewhat less exaggerated in "tilt," early spring flowers, and roses—in very natural tints—being the favourite form of trimmings. Handkerchiefs.—The craze for tiny handkerchiefs continues, and leading shops have found the task of disposing of their stock of medium-sized varieties a difficult one. At the sales these larger handkerchiefs are being offered at a very tempting price; and the industrious home worker will find it advantageous to secure some, reduce them in size, and border them with lace. A very fashionable edging is the American work, which wears splendidly, and is moreover particularly pretty and dainty in appearance.

STOCKTON HEATH.

AGED PEOPLE'S TREAT.—On Monday 96 of Stockton Heath's aged poor were entorteined to tea by Mr. Veto B. Davies and Mr. L. G. Davies, the proceedings being under the superintendence of Mrs. Pigot and the district visitors and other ladies. The tea was followed by an excellent entertainment,the arrangements for which had been made by Mr. F. Rose, who also acted as accompanist on the piano. The members of St. Thomas's Church choir, gave carols, Mr, Arthur Down, gave with skill a couple of ocarina solos, Miss L. Bradshaw sang in a vary pleasing and artistic manner, while lantern views (explained by Mr. Rose) were shown by Mr. John Hallows. The humorous element was sustained by Mr. Alfred Grounds and, as usual caused considerable amusement.

• The Rev. H. V. Pigot, for six years vicar of St. Thomas' Church, Stockton Heath, was on Monday pre- sented with a purse of gold (containing RN), a reading lamp, and an illuminated address, on the occasion of his leaving for Grappenhall where he succeeds the late Rev. Richard Greenell as Rector. The presentation was made during an interval in the proceedings at the old peoples' treat which was held in the Church Institute. . _

Corporation. Improvements. The Warrington Corporation have made an applica- tion to the Local Government Board for a Provisional Order to authorize the Corporation to put in force the powers of the Land Clauses Acts with respect to the purchase and taking of lands otherwise than by agree- ment with reference to the following property, vv.: Land and piemises on the North side of Bylands- street, in the respective occupations of John Oriffiths and Co. and Agnes Williams, and sheds in the rear thereof ; land; stores, stable, yard, and premises adjoining the last mentioned premises and a plot of land on the North side of Manchester-road running in an easterly direction from Number 324, Manchester- road, for a distance of 70 yards or thereabouts. A reply has been received to the effect that the applica- tion has been aceeded to, but the date of the inquiry will not he fixed for two or three weeks.

WOMAN'S REALM.

DRESS - I? A (A I ITO N -HOM E.

THE GE37IAN CROWN PRINCESS.

The Germans are a slow, shrewd, and critical people. To win their good graces is a matter of difficulty. That their Crown Princess, in the space of the few short months which have elapsed since her .marriage should have succeeded in do- ing so, speaks volumes for her, writes Lady Mary in the Woman at Home. Previous to her marriage the Duchess Cecilie of Mecklenburg had lived in retirement, and had ,seldom been permitted to take part in any Court functions. It wns at her brother's marriage with our own Queen's niece, Princess_ Alexandra of Cumber:

land, that thc Crown Princ first met and fell in love with her. Prettier, far, than she is shown in a photograph, the Princess has thick chestnut hair, and a pale face lit with dark ,in- telligent eyes. Her vivacity is remarkable, and it is this girlish animation of hers which has won the hearts of the people. Devoted to - out- door games, the Princess is first rate at tennis; drives and rides . well; and can sail her own yacht. She speaks English nicely and has many English- friends. Those who saw the young bride's entry into Berlin describe it as the nrettiest of all royal 16tes. The happy girl, her head wreathed with roses, and wearing a rose- embroidered - frock, drove through rose arched and garlanded streets; rose-wreaths were flung at her; the air was full of the scent of the flowev. It waos a case of roses, roses, all the way.

DELAINE BtoriE.

Our sketch is taken from a delaine blouse hav- ing a pale-blue ground and deep cherry-red touches in the pattern. It is made with a yoke, on to which the " skirts " are gathered with several rows of gathering threads. A V-shape is cut away in the yoke, to shew a front of tuoked blue silk, which peeps out also between the

vandykes down the front. A binding of the same blue silk, bearing tiny black buttons, edges the front, and is again used on the long cuffs of the sleeves. Most morning blouses are now finished with a linen collar of some description, fastened with a tiny knot or bow in front.

Foil MOTORING.

Motor wraps for the winter seem to occupy the minds of modern womanhood, says M.A.P. Smart Society has of late taken kindly to petrol landaulettes for use in London as well as in the country; and, when driving in these, one 'mist be well wrapped in warm but not necessarily heavy clothing. Some women use their long sealskins, while others prefer nmsquash, a fur of much the same colour and texture, but of far more enduring qualities. Others, again, choose coats made of good cloth lined with fur, and having collars and cuffs Of some better and darker fur. For example, a useful coat might be built of brown cloth, lined with squirrel, and the cuffs and collar of mink or Canadian sable, as even a rich woman would avoid using priceless sable on a coat internied for motoring. And another good garment could be formed out of dark grey cloth, lined with moleskin, and having cuffs and collar Of skunk fur. These coats should be made double-breasted, with some deep pockets, and large, loose sleeves that will slip on easily over the much-trimmed bodice of a winter costume. This get-up should be completed by a fur clip to match, a veil, good gloves and a pair of high, well-made velvet boots.

===== 0002601_19060106_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
CHARMING HOUSE FROCK.

A charmingly simple house frock is illustrated in our sketch. The material of which it is fashioned is dark-green cashmerb, in one of the lovely new weaves which are so supple and drape so admirably. A suggestion of the fashionable Princess robe is conveyed by ,the long panel from bust to hem in front. But though the gown ap- pears to be made in one, it is, in reality, a bodice and skirt. The panel on the skirt is cut with the extra bodice piece in one, and when the skirt is put on it is simply hooked into place on

the bodice. Above this panel appears a dainty vest of lace, from which is turned back a double sailor collar of cashmere and guipure. The bodice is slightly pleated, arid the suggestion of pleating is further carried out in the skirt. A breast knot and folded belt of green panne add richness to the costume; but the oily other trimming con- sists of a little machine-stitching and some green panne buttons on the panel and leg-of-mutton sleeves.

THE CHAUFFEUSE.

A Miss Jane Larkins has qualified herself for the lucrative profession of instructress to ladies who wish to learn how to manage and drive their own motor-cars Miss Larkins, who went through a course of practical study at a London motor company's works, is the best motor-woman in Great Britain, and may be remembered as the clever lady who steered a motor-boat so .suc- cessfully in the trials in Southampton Water last. year. TO-DAY'S RECIPE.

CONSOMME OF PHEASANT.—Rentove .the hest parts of the meat from a cold cooked pheasant-- about of meat will suffice—and put on all the bones and trimmings • (after crushing the former) to boil in about a quart of good stock. adding a leek, an onion, a few sticks of celery. finely minced, some scraps of lean ham or bacon, and A bunch of sweet herbs, after it has come to the boil. Skim the liquid well, and let it simmer vently at the 'side of the fire for about two hours, then strain it through a cloth and re- move the fat. In the meanwhile, mince the meat of the pheasant finely. put it in a mortar, moisten it with a little stock, add the yolks of four eggs, and rub it through a fine hair-sieve, adding a little seasoning to taste. Poach this mixture in a tin lined with buttered natter. and when it is ttri:u put it on one slue to get con", then turn it out and cut' it into small square; like custard: put these into a hot soup tureen and pour the soup on to them, after clarifying it in the usual manner if necessary.

Warrington and Broughton Rangers met in the return match at Broughton on Saturday. Unfortunately for the visitors, Isherwood was compelled to stand down at the last minute, Brookes filling the vacancy. The weather was fine but cold, and a crowd of about 5,000 assembled. Teams : _ _

_ Broughton Rangers—Poole, back; [Hogg, NV. James, C. James, and Harris, threP-quarter backs ; S. James and Horton, half ineks ; Trotter. Smith, Beethsca Whitehead, Ruddick, Fitzsimmons, Winskill and Preston, forwards. Referee, Mr. J. Oakland, Wakefield. Preston started for Warrington, but the home team were the first to attack, and Harris was almost over, Knight pushine him into touch a few yards from the line, Eventually from a scrummage in the Warrington "25 " Sam James got possession, and scored a soft try underthe posts, the Warrington men being under the impression that the whistle had been blown. From an dray position Harris landed a goal. The visitors made one or two efforts to get through, but Poole was very safe. Fish once got away but lacked speed to complete the effort. At the interval the Bangers led by 5 points to nil. . _

On resuming, Warrington showed up better, and Lloyd had a rare chance of reducing Broughton's lead, but he was tackled a few yards from the line. Sam James by brilliant work, was the'rneans of placing the visitors on the defence,and Knight had:all his work cut out to save. Another strong attacking movement was spoiled by Hockenhell, who secured from a scrum near the line and passed widly out of reach of his centres. In the closing stages of the game Broughton pressed strongly, and from a scrummage on the line the ball was punted out by Fish. Poole fielded the ball, and taking true aim dropped a splendid goal. Result :—Broughton Rangers 2 goals, I try (7 points), Warrington nil.

WARRINGTON EISTEDDFOD.

Great Success of Monday's Festival.

The promoters of Warrington's fourth annual Eisteddfod, which to AK place in the Parr Hall on New Year's Day, have every reason to congratulate them- selves on the success of the festival. It speaks ' volumes for local musical enterprise that the public has been educated to a higher appreciation of the value of the eisteddfod. The attendance on Monday was considerably larger than at any previous festival, and the greatest interest was manifested in the com- petitions. This is certainly encouraging, and should stimulate those who have the worthy protect in hand to even greater efforts in the future. In the number of entries the eisteddfod showed a distinct advance, and some good contests resulted. The one great disappointment of the day was that the majority of the male voice choirs which had entered for the chief choral competition failed to put in an appearance. There was, however, some compensation in the fact that the Warrington choir attained a highly creditable victory on which they were cordially congratulated. A new feature which proved popular last year—the brass band contest— was retained and again constituted one of the greatest attractions. Although to the general public it was perhaps monotonous to hear the same piece repeated lby four bands, the contest was followed with the keenest interest by bandsmen and other enthusiasts and the success of Crosfield's band was agreed to ba . fully merited. If any fault could be found with the programme, it was that the great variety of items pleasing as they all were—prolonged the afternoon proceedings very considerably beyond the anticipated time. When the evening meeting should have been commencing—at 6.30 the band competition had not concluded, and it was after seven o'clock when the programme was entered upon. Cansidering the exceptionally large number of entries, howe‘ter, the eisteddfod was very expeditiously conducted and was thoroughly enjoyed by all. Locol vocalists were well to the fore and obtained several prizes, much to the satisfaction of the audiences. The musical adjudicators were Mr. Etnlyn Davies, A.R.C.A., who is well-known to Warringtotnans as a vocalist of great ability, and Mr. J. Whewall, the famous choir conductor. Their task was a heavy one, for from 11 a.m. to 10.30 p.m. they were engaged, almost continuously, in deciding upon the merits of the competitors in the various sections. Mr. James Brier acted as adjudicator in the band contest. As usual the meetings were conducted by the Rev. W. Wynn Davies, who by his geniality and tact succeeded in keeping the audiences in very good humour. Mr. Ernest Wilkes, A.L.C.M., was an efficient accompanist. The success of the eisteddfod was largely due to the admirable arrangements made by the committee, consisting of the following : —Mr. Nathan Cook (chairman), Messrs. G. Ande non, W. Critchley, A. Greenough P. J. George, A. Molyneux, C. Nurse, W. Woodcock, George Morrall (hon. treasurer), R. W. Cask (hon. secretary). Special praise must be accorded to Mr. R. W. Cook for his willing and valuable services as hon. secretary.

AFTERNOON MEETING. PIANOFORTE SOLO COMPETITION.

The esisteddfod opened with this competition, con- fined to children not exceeding twelve years of age. ! The test piece was" Scherzo in B flat " (Schubert) and the competitors selected to appear before the audience were :—Arthur Greenough, S. Reynolds, and Miss Gardner, all of Warrington. The prize was awarded to Arthir Greenough, Willis-street, War- rington, whom the adjudicators stated had a very nice touch indeed. He made one or two slight mistakes but kept better time, showed a better concep- tion of the piece, and brought out the expression more ' effectively than the other competitors.

- - WORKS GLEE COMPETITION. This competition is deserving of special commendation as it undoubtedly tends to encourage music amongst the employs of the local works. Four choirs entered, I the test piece being the well known " Comrades' song of hope" (Adam). The award (£3 and gold centre medal) was won for the third year in succession by Fletcher, Russell and Company's party uuder the con- ductorship of Hr. W. Woodcock. The adjudicators said the successful choir commenced the piece beauti- fully, showing good tone and quality of voices which blended better than in the other choirs. The expres- sion was good and the singers had a better conception of the piece. At one time the intonation was not quite clear, but altogether the performance was very much in advance of the others. The marks were allocated as follows :—Fletcher, ltuesell ad Co., 54 out of possible GO; Co-operative Employees (conductor, Hr. A. E. 'Crow), 52; St. Helens Cable Co. (Hr. P. J: George), 51; D. Johnson's employees, 51. GIRL'S CHALLENGE SOLO. This was a very interesting competition, the girls who appeared in the anal test being Eva Dunbavitelialton Hay •Isherwood Stockton Heath, and Violet Brown, Warrington. In his adjudication, Mr. Whewall highly i commended the singing of Eva Dunbevin, who gained i the first prize. She gave" The Children's Home.' and I Mr. Whewall said he had heard adult singers who did not lame half the feeling into their interpretations of the song that this compet:tor had introduced. She had ! a sympathetic voice of full tone, and her expression was beautiful. Nothing seemed to be wanting except a few more years training, and he thought the girl had a future before her. 'Alley Isherwood was a very artistic singer, with a sweet and sputpathetie voice. Her con- ception of the song" Angels ever bright and fair " was extremely good for one so young. Violet Brown, sang "A May day morning.' The adjudicator said there was a slight drop in the pitch at the end of certain phrases, and she was slightly out of tune at the close of the song: Her words were very good, but not quite so clearly enunciated as in the case of the other competitors. In announcing his decision Hr. Whewall by mistake awarded the prize (ss.and a medal) to May Isherwood. On discovering the error later, he handed the winner ss. from his own pocket and the committee provided another medal.

EVENING MEETING.

PIANOFORTE SOLO COMPETITION.

The hall was utmost filled for the evening meeting The first item was a pianoforte solo competition for children between the ages of twelve and sixteen. The test piece was" Pas des Echarpes " (Chaminade), and the competitors appearing in the final were T. Brother- ton, Bewsey-road, and Ellen Troop, Widnes. The prize was awarded to Ellen Troop, who, the adjudicators stated, kept better time and played with moreexpression than her fellow-competitor.

SECOND CHORAL COMPETITION.

There were only two entries for this competition which was confined to church or chapel choirs—namely, Bewsey-road Wesleyan choir (conductor Mr. B. Wadsworth), and iLegh-street Primitives ((r. A. Spruce). Gonnod's beautiful anthem "Come unto Him" was the test piece. The adjudicators decided that the Bewsey-road choir gave the best interpretation. The balance of tone, they stated, was very satisfactory. The opening was a little too slow and at one stage the intonation was not quite clear. The performance on the whole was very good. Legh-street choir commenced in better time but the tune was not quite as good as with Bewsey-roai, neither was their ending of the piece. The marks awarded were: Bevrsey-road, 55 ; Legh- street, 54.

TENOR SOLO COMPETITION. For the best interpretation of "An evening song" (Blumenthal) the prize was won by Mr. J. Willett, Warrington. The adjudicators said he possessed a good voice and although he sang a little too loud at times ho had a better finish and more artistic style than the other competitors. Mr. F. Edwards, of Ashton, had not his voice so well in command. The other contestant—a St. Helens vocalist—gave the best opening of the three but fell off later. AT.E QUARTET comeEn/r [ON. _ .

, The piece selected for this competition was "Long day closos" (Sullivan) and the marks were allocated as follows :—Mr. C. Rigby and party, St. Helens, 54; Mr. .1. Willett and party, Warrington, 52 ; Mr. Marsden and party, Wigan, 49.

===== 0002601_19060106_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
CHALLENGE SOLO COMPETITION.

This was One of the most interesting features of the day, the soloists having the option of giving any sons of their own choice. Mr. E. Pearse, of Warrington, was the winner, the adjudicators speaking highly of his rendering of " Revenge." He had, they stated, an excellent voice very well adapted to that florid class of music. The slurs were well brought out in very diffi- cult passages and the slow movement was exceedingly well done. Altogether his performance was very credit- able. Mr. Arthur Cheetbam, of Wigan, sang" My hope is in the Everlasting," but the adjudicators said his voice production was somewhat" throaty," and the top notes were therefore too open. If his voice were rightly set he would make a dangerous competitor on any platform. Mr. C. Rigby, of St. Helens, sang" She alone charmeth my sadness." The adjudicators said his voice was well produced and well balanced but it was somewhat too light for the heavy aria occurring in the piece. CONTRALTO SOLO COMPETITION.

-„„- ...... I Sullivan's " Lost chord" was the song chosen for this contest and the vocalists appearing on the platform were Miss Shaw, of Prescot, Miss Potts, of Crewe, and Miss L. Plinston, Warrington. The prize was won by ! Miss Plinston, the adjudicators stating that her articula- tion and finish were clear and she had a good conception of the song. She had a sympathetic voice and although it was not so deep as one of the other competitor's it was better in the higher notes. Miss Shaw's rendering was on the whole, effoctive but a little more tenderness of yoice was required. Miss Potts was a very conscien- tious singer, and her rendering was of the kind that appealed to one. She was apparently somewhat nervous

CIIIIiF CITORAL COMPETITION. Sevfm entries had be"n received for this competition but only two choirs—Wigan Harmonic (conductor, Mr. R. A. Moss) and Warrington (Mr. W. S. Nesbitt)— actually competed. Naturally this robbea the contest of much of its interest, but nevertheless the two render- ings of "Crossing the plains " (Price) were much appreciated. The Warrington choir are to be heartily congratulated on winning the prize of £l2 12s. and a silver cup (presented by Mr. J. Pinlayson). Mr. Whewall said they showed a much better conception of the piece than the Wigan choir. There was a better blend of voices, more expression, good time, and the attack was excellent. With regard to the Wigan choir, their opening WAS very good with the exception that the first tenor was inclined to be flat. Some notes did not receive their full valne. He also suggested that the first basses should be slightly augmented as they were not sufficiently clear. A forcing of tone was evi- dent. The pleasure of the evening was considerably enhanced by Mr. Emlyn Davies's expressive renderings of" When I survey "and" Devotion."

LODGER'S THEFT.

At Warrington ot-i-Tai,;-riTai Jane Smith, Ellen-street, was charged with stealing from the e welling-house of Richard Billington, during the months of November and December, five trinkets and other articles of the value of £3.—She pleaded guilty .—The Chief ConstaLle said the prisoner lodged with the prosecutor's wife, and she was from time to time alone in the house.—Occa- sionally she abstracted various articles of clothing and pledged them. They were not missed until recently, when information was given to the police. Superinten- dent Monaghan apprehended the prisoner, and a number !of tickets wore found in her possession. fhere were ' several convictions against her. She had no need to steal, but she did so to enable her to get drunk. Her husband was in regular employment.—Prisoner, who ; said she was very sorry, was sent to gaol for a month with hard labour.

"When my little girl was a few months old, her eyes began to get bad; thinking it was from her teeth, I lived in hopes of them getting better, but as they did not improve I decided to get medical advice. I took her to the Hospital several times, and subsequently to the Children's Hospital at —, to which institution I took her for twelve months. THE EXAMINER.

JANUARY 6th 1906.

ro-day the 10,365 voters THE ELECTION in the Warrington Parhal. ADDRESSES. mentary division, will have before them the election addresses of the two candidates who seek to represent them in the next Parliament,—Mr. A. H. Crosfield and Mr. Robert Pierpoint. Both will well repay thoughtful perusal, at the conclu- sion of which we thiek that the majority of the electors will have their conviction that the country has had more than enough of Tory mis-rule immeasurably strengthened. As a rule, election addresses do not afford a great deal of guidance to the individual voter. Generally,both emphasize points which are calculated to appeal to a large number of politicians, but in the instance afforded by the addresses before us, we think that this is a conspicuoasly absent feature. Mr. Crostield's address is a thoroughly progressive compendium of present-day politics, couched in trenchant, unhesitating, and convincing language. On the other hand, that of Mr. Pierpoint is as colourless as—may we say it with all due respect—the ' periodical address from the Throne to the Houses of Parliament. In fact, it has even less individu- ality and force in its composition, and a weaker' appeal to the electorate from a party represents- 1 tive we do not remember to have read. Mr. Crosfield rightly devotes himself larAtly to the two great issues which have split the thinking section of the people of this country completely asunder. There are Free Traders and Protectionists ; there are advo cotes of Chinese labour in South Africa, and opponents to the same. We have, we believe, the most ample and all-sufficient reasons for feel- ing convinced that the majority in each case is unmistakably on the side of Free Trade and "no slavery" not only all over the country but in Warrington also. The Liberal candidate leaves no room for doubt as to where he stands in regard to both these questions so vital to the well-being and the self-respect of Englishmen. Mr. Crosfield says "I stand as a FREE TRADER "and "I feel most strongly that the introduction of this (Chinese Labour) into South Africa is an utter ateamination." No expression of opinion could be more direct or uncompromising, and we believe all the more enlightened electors in Warrington will thank him in their hearts for his outspoken avowal. Mr. PIERPOINT is any- thing but explicit on the first of these questions, and ingenuously omits any allusion to the second. But of this more anon. There is a third great issue more or less—but rather less than more—in the minds of the people to-day. That is, Home Rule for Ireland. It owes no present prominence to the fact that it is at all likely to become one of the pressing measures of the new government, but solely because the Tories, with that ingeniousness in befogging the public mind for which they have always been famous, have drawn it across the trail of Free Trade and Chinese Labour. The most ardent Home Rulers do not press for the realisa- tion of their aims at the present juncture; they are content to wait until the Fates, and the state of English politics, are more propitious. But the Tories profess to see a real, and imminent danger to the Empire in Home Rule at the present moment. In their own foolish- ness they are must certainly the only people in England who do. Apart from the merits of the Home Rule proposal, it is not a matter for public concern just now at all. It is one of those matters that, like Micawber, can wait. Mr. CROSFIELD might, with the best excuse, have refrained from alluding to it. But with a straight- forwardness which does him credit he says plainly " I hold that in Ireland, as in England, govern- ment should be of the people, for the people, and by the people.'" With this assurance the matter may safely be dismissed for the present by both Home Rulers and anti-Home Rulers. Neither side can prejudice its case by turning directly to those causes which have rent the country in twain, and by considering more closely what the last ten years of Tory rule have cost us as a nation. "Behind us," as Mr. Crosfield says, "lie ten years of re-action, unnecessary wars and rumours of wars, extravagance, and Tory class legislation," and in the forefront is the I baleful figure of Protection raising its hydra-head over a peaceful community. The Liberal candi- date touches also upon the Taxation of Land Values—one of the most pressing of domestic reforms—and judge-made law as affecting Trades Unions. His views on these questions are too well- known to need recapitulation here, and we think it may safely be concluded that when tha electors of Warrington give him the opportunity, he will put all the strength and energy and ability he possesses on the side of the democracy in the struggle for right against might as exemplified in the status quo of these two, and many other, thorny problems. Mr. PIERPOINT'S address is unmistakably the effort of a man who has little to say, but is deter- mined to occupy an imposing amount of space in saying it. The patient elector will have to wade through two-thirds of its length before he strikes any reference to those questions on which the election of Monday, the 15th inst., will be fought. The Tory nominee wares quite eloquent on some of the things the late Government have done in the way of home legislation, and in regard to

===== 0002601_19060106_art0134.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====
'

foreign policy. But he says nothing about those things which have been left undone and those which have been done badly. We cannot blame him for this—it is not his business—but he must not be resentful if a wakeful and discrimin- ating public fails to enthuse with him about prosperity in Egypt and an assured peace in the Soudan. Since great GORDON died, we have not been much concerned with Soudinesa affairs, and they probably rank in public estimation no higher thim Cyprus—that"foreign"island enshrined in Mr. PIERPOINT'S affections. He does, however, make a brief reference to the questions of Licens- ing and Education, and asserts that they have been dealt with " in a manner which has given general satisfaction to all fair-minded people.' The conclusion is evident from the state of public feeling in the country that" fair-minded people" are in a very large minority. Those who are not " fair-minded " but constitute the great majority 1-iok upon the LICENSING Acr of 1904 as a Brewers' Endowment Act, which has created a vested interest in the licence. They recollect that Mr. ACE E rts-DouGLAs, in introducing the Bill, spoke of one clause as " a special safeguard to the Trade," and they are con- vinced now that in safeguarding the trade, the Government entirely overlooked the interests of the community as a whole. The EDUCATION Acr is a piece of class legiEla.tion which has destroyed the School Boards, retained religious tests for the rappointment of teachers, saddled great parts of the country—Warrington included—with heavy ' rates, and created such religious bitterness and administrative confusion as militate against real progress in education. CHINESE Linoun, as have intimated above, is the one great question about which our late member says " nuffin. , Evidently he realises its unpopularity with the masses, and the difficulty of stating a case for the slavery ordinance. We have no need to sum up. Mr. PIERPOINT can find nothing to say why, after we plunged into a disastrous war, costing £250,000,000 and 2,5,000 British lives, to "obtain political freedom " for the Uitlanders, we sub- stituted Chinese coolies—herded in compounds under conditions tantamount to slavery—for the existing labour and to the exclusion.,of white miners. Judgment must go by default against the late government and its representative in War- rington, and not even their humiliation should , lessen the severity of the verdict to be pronounced on Monday the 15th. 1, With regard to the FISCAL QUESTION, Mr. PIERPOINT dare not remain silent, or we believe Ihe would have done so. In the light of the devel- opments of the last two years our late member's position must have been the cause of ninth gloomy personal retrospect. When Mr. CHAMBERLAI raged rampant through the country with a plea for undisguised Protection, embodying taxes on such necessaries as foreign corn, meat, eggs, butter, c heese, and fruit, Mr. PlElll'Ol/I T was bold and declared himself a whole-hogger. Mr. , CHAMBERLAIN said "he believed" these taxes would be paid by the foreigner, but he failed to ' persuade the country that the taxes would not !, raise the prices to the consumer of all these coin.. ; modities. Protection has since declined in public t favour ; and on economic grounds,too,has had the bottom knocked out of it so thoroughly that there is not a stave left. Mr. Pr RRPOINT has lain low, until recently, when, as in his election address to-day, all he commits himself to is a conference with the Colonies for mutual perference. He has pluck than his leader, who still sticks to a good deal of his original scheme. We people of Warrington to take note 0., end remem- ber that, on November 6, 1903, speaking at the Junior Conservative Club, Mr. PIERMINT said, in alluding to Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S proposals that :

WO

"He could if he liked make his observa- tions extremely short by simply saying Them's my sentiments.' As to the question •of the Fiscal conditions, Mr. Chamberlain had put forward a remedy and, in his opinion, what Mr. Chamberlain proposed was the best policy not only for this country, but for the empire." Does the nation want Mr. CHAMBERAIN'S policy of fiscal "reform "and aear food? We say, No ! Does Warrington want Mr. PIERPOINT with his colourless representation, with his wavering con- victions on the fiscal pestion, and with his opinionless attitude on Chinese Labour? We Itrust the answer will be as emphatic a negative. Finally we exhort every voter to remember that every vote given at the last elec- tion was a vote for a tax ou bread, dearer sugar, the destruction of the school boards, the endow- ment of the brewing trade, and class legislation of the worst type. Ten years of Toryism has cost us 42 millions a year more in taxes than under the last .Liberal Government. Vote solidly therefore, for CROSFIELD. Free Trade, Cheap Food, No Slavery. Liberty of Conscience, and Democratic Legislation!

The Liberal campaign could , THE LIBERAL not have opened more en- I CAMPAIGN. couragingly. Workers are rallying round the Free Trade standard in ever-increasing numbers, and in every direction the greatest enthusiasm pre- . veils. The two meetings already held have been : i splendidly attended and not a single dissentient , 'note has been raised. Up to quite recently a Liberal meeting at Latchford was almost " 1 unknown, but when Mr. Crostield opened his campaign at that end of the constituency : on Wednesday evening the, Parish Room , was filled with an audience which seemed , to thoroughly realize the gravity of the issues at stake and to be determined to resist the attempt to undermine Free Trade. Thursday's meeting in the Co-operative Hall, Winwick•street, was one of the finest ever held by the party in that quarter. ' A. Working class audience crowded the building to forceful sallied! dr e shadaddress ho ea d emaol ts most i th cordialth e 1 actl , I‘ifor. rc. overflowingreception. I two great issues on which the fight will turn Free Trade and Chinese labour and his views appeared to fully express the sense of the meeting. Free Prade he pointed out, gives British manufacturers an enormous advantage over Protectionist nations ' as owing to the unlimited supply of untaxed raw , materials which our present policy ensures, they : are able to produce more cheaply than their cone- ' petitors and beat them in the great neutral markets of the world. His authoritative information on t the exports of the cotton and soap industries were particularly convincing on this point. With regard to : Chinese labour, he showed that the system could ; only be accounted a species of slavery and that the roineowners' statements proved that they feared the political rights which British labour would demand. Mr. NV. Midden,who is an eloquent and convincing speaker, is rendering yeoman service . _ to Catln

_ the party. Coming from. a staunch Irish lobo his words should carry weight, particu- ly among his own community. The two meet- s form a happy augury for the success of cause, and there is every indication that husiasm will be maintained until the tight is

The foreign labour cry FOREIGN LABOUR. as raised against Mr. . Crosfield Warrington is amusing considering the source from which it emanates. 'ln the distorted vision of our Tory friends it looks horribly wrong .for the Liberal candidate to employ two or three expert foreigners in the development. of his business, and gloriously right for the late Tory Government to introduce 47,000 foreign (Ohinese) coolies into the South African mines in order that the Randlord may obtain manual labour at is. per day and raise his dividends to anything between 50 per cent and 200 per cent. The spectacle in Warrington must bea source of vast amesem i 3nt to the work- ingman fhe has any sense of humour. That he is likely to be turned from the really great issues before him by such petty personalities we do not for a moment believe. it must he as.plain to him as the nose on his fa that the Torie§ are hard-up for an election cry. They have no arguments to advance why Mr. Pierpoint and the remnant of a , party be represents. should be returned to power. Home rule has been raised as a bogey to frighten the electors with, hut they hay° been too impatient to look at it. Now it is "foreign " labour as emnloyed by Mr. Crosfield. The silly canard has been already exploded so far as Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Ltd, are concerned, and we don't see how the Liberal candidate is going to be saddled to any further extent with such a heinous crime. He nifty possibly have employed a foreign " " when golfing in France, but the difficulty of importing the Ecirlish variety on ta the French links must he held sufficient excuse. We have beard, ton, that Mr. Pierpoint, our own immacu- late member who finds so troech employment in the town, has been known to employ a " foreianer " to carry his luggage when on some interesting P xen rsion in Cyprus. The rumour may he false. We hope it is for the sake of the party which would be so humiliated by such a revelation in regard to the foreign labour for which it has suddenly developed a new-found hatred.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

Never mind fairy tales about foreign labour. Vote for the man who will represent Warrington and not Cyprus. * * * We are getting nearer the light in other ways besides politically. On Wednesday, the earth reached its nearest approach to the sun. They were only 91,338,000 miles apart. * * Colonel Wyndham Quin,* the Tory candidate for South Glamorgan, has for a month pa•A been con- gratulating his audiences on the good already achieved by the Aliens Bill. It has just been pointed out that the Bill only came into operation on New Year's Day, and the Colonel is now feeling somewhat alienated ! * * *

"The coming election carries in its lap graver con-

.sequences than any election since 1852. Tbe nation has to say whether the food of the people shall be taxed,whether the lot of the poor and the unemployed, hard as it is, shall be made harder by iniquitous legislation in the interest of privileged classes."— Lord Welby. * * Mr. A. J. King, the Liberal candidate for the KnutsFord Division, is addressing meetings to-day , (Saturday) fit the schoolroom, Stratton, and the school- room Appleton, the first at 7 p.m. and the second at 7'3° P.m. The Chairman at the first meeting will be b Mr. John Darhyshire, and at the second, Mr. Henry Roerts,

* There MIR a case for the R.S.P.C.A. in Sankey-street on New Year's day. One pf the shining lights of the Junior Conservative Association was seen leading a , large collie dog, hali-stran,gled with the blue ribbons f J. .or a card bearing the touchino' inscription" Pierpoint or ever!' We know now why some Conservative canvassers appear to have no more persuasive power -than dumb beasts.

* * •Nolnithstanding, that Mr. Lloyd George has just been down to Carnarvon Boroughs and taken his con- stituency by storm, Mr, R. A. Naylor still perseveres with his candidature. Speaking at Bangor the other day he said : "At first I was ridiculed, then I was . abused and now lam threatened with serious bodily harm,'brit let me teltyon that I would face death for 'the working man." 'Bravo, R.A.N. ! You are braver 'than your initials Would suggest. . , *s* Mr. John Hodge, Labour candidate for Gorton : , They could not have an earthly paradise out of a fiscal pill, otherwise Russia would be happy. The earth is the Lords' and the fulness thereof —the House of Lords. Warrington Drowning Fatality. Mr. S. Brighouse, coroner, held an inquest at the Warrington Police Station on Thursday morning on the body of John Woods, aged seventy-four, of 234 Liverpool-road, whose body was found in the Sankey and St. Helens Canal, near Messrs. Clare and Ridg- way's shipbuilding yard, on Monday, The evidence showed that the deceased was last seen going to his work on Monday afternoon, and he then appeared in his usnal spirits. As he did not return, the police wore informed, and a search resulted in Police-con- stable Houghton finding his body in the canal.—A verdict of "Accidental death" was returned. At Warrington on Thursday, 'John White, 19, Gas- Street, was charged with stealing from a cart in Golden-square a portmanteau containing numerous articles of clothing to the value of :CI3 is. (id., the property of Mr. J. Rymer Young, J.P. The Chief Constable said that on Wednesday a carrier from Lynun called at Mr. Yomtg's house in Palmyra-square to convey a portmanteau to Lymm. Another call had to be made by the carrier in Golden Grove-street, off Lyme-street. The man left his cart unattended, and when lie came hack the portmanteau was missing. In- formation was given to the police, and prisoner was apprehended by Superintendent Monaghan on suspic- ion. The offence was first denied, but a search at the house of prisoner's mother in Gas-street revealed the stolen property hidden in the boiler and other places The magistrates sent prisoner to gaol for six months with hard labour, and Superintendent MOriaghan was complimented for his smartness in the capture*

manufactured goods. 2 per ty on corn, and 5 per cent duty oe food stuffs r.:ue bacsen and maize. As long as he holds to thi., r atectiaie policy It is wasting words to think of this risEst.han it is. Disguise it as you like it is Protection.,.n.l n thing else. The attraction that his Ickes .1 to many minds draws its strength horn two er three elementary errors First, that providiug of wait as an end in itself. Ssoond that the monist o • work to be dons in a country is a fixed quantity; and l This theory be true it follows should foreign made geods come in, they lessen the work to be ..lone,and shrel.. a foreigner come in and do coins of thE work, he taker one of the work and bread from some ),liish Tcqd;lar. Both these assumptions are erroneous, but upon these two rotten props Protection, now as ever, mainly rests. .If it were true that our object should be to make work, Protectionism would and should hold the (tell It is the ism that toschesthat waste work makes wealth. But it is not work we want, except in so far it procures us the products of work, and it is the want of apprecia- tion of the fact that work is only valuable as a means to an end which makes the confusion and difference between those who usually agree. If work were ,an end, then we might solve all difficulties by putting men to dig holes and fill them no again. This looks very absurd, but it only differs in degrees from the absurdity of diverting energy from trades which prosper without stimulus, into trades made prosnerous by alO per cent. duty. This duty is designed to make lus do for ourselves what other countries Cla do for us 'cheaper by 10 cent, and which they exchange for these things which it pays us better to make. The work spent in doing things which others are willing to do for us involves waste and the measure of that waste is roughly the tariff encouraging such waste. Mr. Chamber- lain referred to our imports of egos wad the eneoerage- meat it would give us to keep liens if his 5 per cent. 'duty were put on. I have tried my hand at egg produc- ing and after a great waste of energy managed to make £lOa year. If the sense energy had been put into leather making it would have produced ten thins the amount. Is it not better that we should build ships, weave cloth, tau leather, roll iron, than we should spend oar energies laying eggs—l beg the hens' pardon—l mean keening hens when other people such as those in llollend are willing to do this work for us ? But in taxing eggs Mr. Chamberlain taxes raw material, for I am informed that about one third of the eggs coming into this country are used in the manufacture of light leathers. This illustrates how difficult it is to draw a distinction between food which he desires to tax, and raw material which he desires to let in free.

A word about the idea that there is only a limited amount of work to he done and therefore we mast keep out foreigners from doing any share of it. This error is twin brother to the absurdity I have spoken of above. As long as h. foreigner does his share of work and by sa doing contributes to the wealth of the' country his presence is a national benefit. A foreigner does not devour the coin he gets as wages, he spends its a con- sumer, lie gives hisl labour, but unless the wealth he produces in ' the form of goods was more valuable to his employer than the money he gets he would not be employed. The wealth he creates is either. spent. or used as capital, which in its tarn employs further labour. ' The only class of foreigners we ought to object to are those who become a burden to society by not con- tributing thsir share to the work of national produc- tion.

—The lump of wark theory is altogether There is no limit to the desires of man, wealth creates desire, desire calls for %yogic to create the objects of desire. If the theory of &limited amount of work were true then every mtchine, every appliance to save work would be a curse. It is very singular that the same arguments which are used to buttress Protection (used by Arr. Chamberlain and all who follow him) were used by those who opposed the introduction of the power loom. There is nothing new in the present day Pro- tection arguments, they are as old as the hills and as false as they are old.

There ought to be a fine gime at Warrington to-day when Leeds mike their annual visit. As a rule they play excellent foothill, and for s nne years now have been one of the mist powerful teams in Yorkshire. This season they have done particularly well, and along with Oldham.and Haaslet are strong, challengers for the championship of the League. LETTERS TO THE EDITOR

CORRESPONDENTS are particularly requested to send letters intended for Friday's " Esaminer " not later than Thursday morning.

" Free Trade Converts."

Sir,—Mr. Pierpoint is reported to have said at the Tory meeting that selected him as candidate last weeo "he did not know of a large business man who hak ' left Toryism for Free Trade doctrines." I could givd Mr. Pierpoint several loaol instances of business men who whilst being" Unionists " were nevertheless Free Traders and intended to vote that way too. To go further afield what about the Right Eion.Baron Ritchie (one of the soundest business men in the late Tory cabinet) who, rather than support Protection fallacies, seceded from the Government ? • Then there is Mr. Frank Calvert. J.P„ of Preston who resigned the chairmanship of the Conservative club on account of Free Trade views. Mr. Calvert is a large manufacturer and prominent Churchman. Mr. Pierpoint quotes Mr. Chamberlain as a keen business ram but forgot to mention he made his business and profits under a Free Trade system. No, I am afraid our late member is wide of the mark when he thinks there are no promi- nent Tory business men identified with Free Trade.— Yours, &c., QUILOOK.

===== 0002601_19060106_art0153.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. F. Monks and Free Trade.

Sir,-11r. F. Nlonks' attempt to minimise the import- ance of the Fiscal issue a the forthcoming election will have mnissed its mark. and is hardly worthy of himself. I would recommend to his consideration the proverb which says, " Vain is the snare of the fowler, set in the sight of any bird." The questions which he asked of the P trlia,mentary candidates were three in number, but the most moment- ous question before the country,viz Toe Fiscal question, was sandwiched between the other two as if it were of minor importance. Yet this very question arose in a quarter from which it might have been least expected if previous professions and declarations were to have any weight, rand it has been distinctly heard again and again by its self-constituted missionary, that it was to have precedence over all others, because this country's very existence as a manufacturing and commerical country, &penes on the verdict. The two candidates have. replied. One, Mr. Pierpoint's he Beeman quite satisfied with because it meets his wishes. The other, Mr. Crosflela's he finds fault with because it does not meet his wishes. One of the grounds of his complaint is that Mr. Crosfield naturally casts a doubt on his constancy to his formerly professed con- victions, at which Mr. Monks protests. Bat those who have known him for upwards of 30 years as I have, hav- ing regard to his public utterances, and to the attitude he nOw assumes, will conclude that like the Dlayer queen in" Hamlet,"" He doth protest two much." I anal not concerned to vie..v. the question from a party standpoint. I frilly agree with the self constituted missionary of so called Tariff Reform, that it is a national one, which ought to be considered apart from party, and that anyone with a spark of patriotism left in his constitution will sink all others in its favour. But these tariff reformers seem now to be afraid of the apparition that their leader has raised, and as this was a "red herrin"dravyn across the trail of another question so they now seek to draw yet another red-herring across thetrai lof this. But the electors will not be so easily hoodwinked.

atr. Aionto says he is a thorough Free Trader, but he understands Free Trade to mean, free to exports as well as to impnrts,therehy implying the worn out,threadbare charge that the deciples of Cobden, are not thorough, but one sided Free Traders. I ens tempted to ask," is Saul also among the prophets ?" But he says further that he is old enough to remember that" this was what the advocates of Free Trade intended." He is then another clairvoyant and knows better what they in- tended than they knew themselves. He then goes on to say," In those days tariffs were for revenue only, and both Cabden and Bright honestly believed that tariffs would be abolished as the world came to see the advan- tage of the free exchange of commodities." 1 claim to know something of the work of these two men and the advocates of Free Trade in general, but I have not seen nnythat would lend me to the conclusions as to th sir intentions that Mr. Monks now declares. I shall there- fore be glad if he will give his warrant or authority for saying that the advocates of Free Trade intended the adoption of free exports as well as free imports. Further " that tariffs in those days," that is, before the abolition of import duties, and the adoption of what he would now call " one sided" Free Tr ide, " were for revenue only," and also that Cabden and Bright. and the Free Tracie League sought the abolition of tariffs all the world over,—Yours faithfully, A. I have so much faith in his faltiness that I feel sure he will not object to being a little more precise. Mutual protection tinder Free trade is one thing ; Fiscal Pro- tection (capital P) is quite another matter. Which does he mean? As to the former we seem to be agreed that it is not only allowable but even nocessary,as much among manufacturers as among workmen, provided it be for proper ends. Take the specific instance, I con- tended that manufacturers were justified in combining to Ile a standard price so as to protect the consumers from deterioration of quality rind their workmen from low wages. They agree to forego certain advan- tages in the game so as to lift the game into is higher level. This is particularly necessary in soap where it is almost impossible for the public tie protect itself by guarantees of purity. but it does not follow that the producers are relieved of competition. A game of billiards may be spot-harried but there is still room for play, and in like manner there is enough competition among our English firms to keep up the qaality of the product. But if home competition were insufficient for this rvirp use or if the manufacturers combined to fix not merely it price lantalso a relatively low quality, there is nothing under our Free Trade system to prevent foreign manufacturers from offering us a better article at the price or as good an article at a less price. Thus by Fret; Trailc the general body of the public is vrot del from combinations made to advance sectional interests.
