NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS. Rugby Football. [By "Ovalist."j

Warrington have started the Nev. Year with a victory. I hope I am justified in considering this a • hopeful augury, for their form during the four con- cluding months of the past year has been too bad to be true. They have shaped more like wooden-spoonists than cup-holders, and the gates have suffered in con- sequence. lam not betraying a very profound secret when I say that the receipts for many of the first-team matches this season have only been half what they were in, the corresponding firtures -a year ago. Injuries to men like Fish, Dickens m, and Isherwood' have had alot to do with this " falling off," but they are not wholly responsible. The men who remain have not shown the ability which they undoubtedly possess, and in point of play are hardly recognisable this season when compared with last. There is a want of enthusiasm too in League matches, which bodes ill for the position of the Club when the programme is completed.

Warrington finished up the old year with about their worst exhibition. They had an excellent chance of success at Broughton where the home team was handicapped by the absence of both centres, but the crude notions of attacking which the Warrington men showed Bayed the Mangers from any serious fears. The passing was atrocious from the halves to the centres, with the result that the wings were mostly Hockenhall and Lloyd certainly don't hit it off, and it was difficult to say which was the worse of the two on Saturday. Both played vary poor football. On the opposing side Sam James was, as US ual, a host in himself. He opened out the gime in fine style, and if the home team had not been so weak at centre, Warrington must have been severely beaten. As it was the total was only 7-0 which is one of the smallest scores recorded at Broughton this season.

There is one eKtennating and remarkable circum- stance to be urged on Warrington's behalf. Less than five minutes from the start the Rangers were in the Warrington quarter, and Sam James secured the ball. He, rounded the "serum" when the whistle suddenly sounded and the Warrington men eased up. James went on, no opposition being offered, and , grounded behind the posts. Then the music started, so to speak. The Warrington men appealed or bloc. , A whistle had certainly been blown at the critical moment. Even the referee admitted it and walked to the rails to try and discover the offender. 'No use of • course. The try was allowed and a goal added. I don't say that it should not have been, but I think the referee might justifiably have ordered the game to be re-started seeing that only about four minutes had ' elapsed. Anyhow, it was a piece of hard luck for the , Warrington men, and the mischievousness of it should be brought home to the Broughton ollicials., Rumour hath it that this is not the first time on which a , mysterious whistle has unaccountably sounded at Wheater's Field.

THE COMING OF 19D6.

A few second 3 after midnight on Sanity the silence lof the "mystic " hour was broken by the sound of ' Cauret be:ls ringing in merry peals combined with the 'discordant hooting of works " buzzers," the shrill ear- splitting shriek of r tilway locomotives and the deep sonorous notes sounded by vessels passing along the great waterway between Liverpool and Manchester. Titus WAS the coming of 1903 heralded locally and right royal was the welcome extended. Weary watchers, their vigil over, hastened to exchange the heartiest greetings and to wish friend and stranger alike a happy and prosperous new year.

WITCH-NIGHT SERVICES.

‘Vatch-night services were held at a number of local churches and at ea3h, as midnight approached the con- gre4ations couli nit f.til to b 3 impressed with, a sense of the solemnity of the occasion.

ST. PAUL'S CEIURCEI. A watch-night service was held at St. Paul's at ll p.m., at which there was every largo congregation. The service W. 13 conducted by the Vicar and his colleague, the ItM,. R. H. 83V1700, The Vicar preached from the words " Par the Son of Mtn is COMB to seek, and to save that which was lost." A few minutes before twelve o'clock the congregation engage i in silent prayer, and then the bell tolled the hour. When the New Year dawned the preicher wished all present" A very happy and prosperols New Year." A collection was made on behalf of the Warrington Enarraary and the District Nursing Association, £7 being realized.

BOLD-STREET. The service at Bold-street Wesleyan Church was con- ducted by the Rev. Joseph &fuming, who chose for his text " Then I want down to the potter's house' and, behold, he wrought a work on the wheels. And the vessel that he made of clay was marred in the hand of the potter: so he made it again another vessel as seemed good to the potter to make it." The rev. gentleman addressing a large congregation. The text taught them a great truth. There was a consciousness that the best had not been made of life, that the things they had longed for had not been reachei. There was the gospel of hope for those who had failed, and God gave them another chance. The new year might be what God needed it to be. He was able to remould them and shape tliern. If they served Him life might become what He meant it to be. The hand of mercy was stretched out to save them. Let them yield to the hand of God that their life daring the new year might be like the vessel shaped by the hand of mercy. A collection was taken in aid 'of Dr. Stevenson's home.

WYCLIFFE CHURCH. The Rev. John Yonge condncted the watch-night service at Wycliffe Church at, which there was a large attendance. In a brief addre=s which be delivered he said there were many subjects to which reference might be made at the close of the old year. When 1905 began two of the greatest empires of the world were at deadly strife, but now peace had been brought about and one, at least, was enjoying the fruits of peace in all calm- Less and delight. The great empire of Russia, about which we all read day by day, was torn by revolution and anarchy. In our own land, too, the year had been eventful in more ways than one. We had recently witnessed a change of Government in the country and we were now on the eve of a great: political contest. In our own town the year had been eventful—eventful particularly in the number of people who had passed away in death, some of them very swiftly and suddenly. In their Wycliffe community it had been a memorable year in a similar way. He had been there 29 years, and he did not know any year in which death had been so busy, so that their sympathies would go out to the many families amongst them who harl saffered loss. Now the year was ending ; they were about to start afresh. Did it strike them that it was an opportune moment to further open their hearts to God ? Would it not be a blessed wty of ending the year now passing, and a splendid way of beginning the year they would soon be welcoming, if they surrendered their heart and soul to God ? Mr. W. J. Barnes sang excellently " Pray, brethren, pray," Mr. Payton presiding at the organ. The passing of the year was spent in silent prayer, and 1901 welcomed with the hearty singing of" Forward be our watchword."

GOLBORNE STREET BAPTIST CHURCH. There was a large congregation at Golborne-street Baptist church on Sunday night the service at 11.15 opening with the hymn" 0 God, our help in ages past." An appropriate sermon was delivered by the Rev. H. Minnie° who based his remarks on the text.. "lint that the world might know that I love the Father and as the Father gave me commandment, even so I do. Arise let us go henna," The preacher at the outset des- cribed the circumstances under which the words of the text were written. They had now, as a congregation, come to the end of the year in which all the dissppoint- , ments.victorie3 and defeats were fast disappearing, and another year of mystery and darkness was coming into view. It was, therefore, appropriate for them to con- sider the words " Arise, let us go hence." If they ex...ainined the verse carefully they would find ' that there were two things in it—the purpose of the summons—a call to go hence. \\That was the call? A call to endurance, suffering, obedience, and revelation. Perhaps some of them might not agree with him when he said th,t when Christ came into the world He did not understand the purpose for which He had done so. It was a revelation to Him afterwards. He was born in Bethlehem, and as He grew up into a youth the great mission dawned upon Him fully, and right through life He felt he had come to do la certain work, and that the one supreme purpose of His life was duty, and never once did he fail to fulfil that duty. Toe preacher commented upon the words, erefurance, sti tforing, obedience and revelation, and said that if they wanted to save their fellow men and serve them —he the preacher) did not care what sphere it was in—the price was suffering endurance, obedience. The words of the text were not " Arise lind go hence," but" Let us "go hence. That meant the companionship of Jesus Christ, who said," La, I ant with you always, even unto the end of the world." They NvA re apt to forget that Christ was their companion, and how many took him into the factory, the workshop, and the counting-house ? They did not know what was in store for them during that year and the succeeding years, but if they had the companionship of Jesus Christ it would lighten their I burden and they would be wonderfully helped. The last moments of the old year, and the first of the new one, were spent in. silent prayer, and the service Iwas concluded by the singing of a hymn.

LORD WELBY AND THE COMING • ISSUE.

The following is a copy of an important letter which Lord Welby has addressed ato the "Cobden Club Circular":—

Internal issensions have broken up the Protectionist party. The Protectionist Government has fallen at last, and a Liberal Government, broadly representative of Liberal opinion, appeals to the nation for a national decision on the issue between Protection and Free Trade.

_ The coming election carries in its lap graver conse- quences than any election since 1552. The nation has to say whether the food of the people shall be taxed. whether the lot of the poor and the unemployed, hard as it is, shall be made harder by iniquitous legislation in the interests of privileged classes. In 1552 Parliament deliberately repudiated the policy of Protection. In 190 G the Protectionist party—the party of doles and privilege—asks the nation to repudiate the policy of Free Trade, and to revert to the system of intricate and provocative tariffs to be framed by ignorant ministers to the profit of powerful producers and lobbying speculators. The author of the new policy is the statesman who has twice destroyed the party to which he belonged,and who has such a contempt for the intelligence of the English people that he has dared to use in support of Protection the very facts and figures which, a few years ago, he used with greater force in support of Free Trade.

It is evident that the Tory party will endeavour at • the coming election to confuse the issue, they will not dare to challenge a direct verdict on their policy of Protection pureand simple. They %ill raise bogies to divert, votes from the main question—is the food of the people to 1)0 taxed ? It is for the nation to see that this transparent device—more truly dodge—shall fail. If the voter is to be frightened by the bogie of Home Rule, let him remember that if the Liberals were to introduce a Home Rule Bill it would find a short shrift in the House of Lords, whereas, if the. Tory party were to obtain a majority, Protective laws, adopted by the House of Commons, would be received with delight by the House of Lords, where Protec- tionists out-number Free Traders by probably 10 to I. The decision between Protection and Free Trade touches the very roots of our national life. It is an issue which concerns the foundation of the moral andimaterial well- being af the people. A small majority in favour of Free Trade will encourage the Tories and the speculators to hope that, at another election, the Liberal Government may hava made mistakes and booome unpopular, that the pendulum will swing back to them and that they may interpret ' such swing back as a vote against Free Trade. In that case Protection will remain in active agitation to disturb and distract our counsels for years to come. On the other hand, a sweeping majority in 1906 will discourage the agitators and teach the Tories that they had better not allow Mr. Chamberlain to lead them again into disaster.

Wycliffe P.S.A. Society.

There was a fair attendance at the meeting of this Society on Sunday when the secretary (Mr. B. Farrar) presided, and drew lessons appropriate to the New Year. Mr. Wolstencroft, of Swinton, gave an address on "Lessons from blind Bartemeus," alluding to Christ's great love to him. Although he hada great affliction and was at a great disadvantage, yet Christ did not overlook him.—Master H. Griffiths sang "Angels ever bright and fair." Mr. D. Owen took part in the meeting. Two new members were cordially welcomed. Mr. W• IL Payton, Mus. Bac., presided at the organ, and appro. priato hymns were sung.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

THE HELSBY STABRING CASE. At Chester Quarter Sessions on Monday, John Frederick Ody 413 j, foreman telephoae engineer, was charged with unlawfully wounding William Henry Hyde, at Helsby, on the 11th November. Mr. Ralph Rankes prosectited,aud Mr. Trevor Lloyd defended. ;Mr. , Baukes said that on the night of the 11th of November prisoner and prosecutor, who were perfectly good ! friends, and had been for five years, had some little ' argument when near their homes,which were in Helsby. There was a slight dispute. The wife of the latter ; came out and asked Ody to go home. Ody struck prose mtorovno did not find out that he bad been stabbed until he got in the kitchen of his house. Hyde was re- moved to Chester Inflrmary in a motor-car, where he was a oatient for about three weeks.—Prosecutor, in his evidence, said he met prisoner at Helsby Club. He (witness) had four glasses of beer. When they arrived home they stood talking near the gate, and after a time witness felt a blow in the stomach, and found when he got into the house that he had been stabbed.—Mrs. Hyde, who saw her husband and prisoner talking at the gate, said prisoner Wes at the time stupidly drunk.—Mr. ; Bryan. who was called to the injured man. gaveparticu- lars of the wound. Counsel for the defence urged that prisoner at the time was so drunk that be did not know what he was doing—Evidence of prisoner's good character was given.—The jury, after a short retirement, found prisoner guilty of unlawful wounding with no intention to do grievous bodily harm."—fhe Chairman said that, in view of vrisoner's good character, the majority of the bench had decided to impose a fine or £5 and costs, or two months' imprisonment, prisoner also to pay the costs of the prosecution.

SFIOP MANAGErt's ALLEGED ESILIEZZLENIENT.

At the Bcrough Police Court on Monday, John Beamont Weatherall, 3, Arpley-street, was charged on a warrant with embezzling the sum of £23 from John Snowden.—Mr. Browne (solicitor) prosecuted on behalf of NEI-. Snnwden. and applied for a remand for a week, as the hooks had not yet bran properly gone through. Prosecutor was a wholesale clothier at Bradford, and in September last he opened a shop in Sankey-street, and employed the prisoner as manager at a salary of 355. a week, with house, coal, and gas free, and 2,1 per cent. commission on the gross takings. The duties of the prisoner were to collect and receive money, busk it each week, and forward to Nlr. Snowden statement. On October 28th last prisonerl sent a statement showing that he had 1--.nked £53 Os. Id., but it was afterwards discovered nothing at all was banked. Unfortunately the prosecutor met with an accident and it was on that account that the deficiencies were not -discovered earlier. It had been found that in three months £270 was missing, and where the money had gone to he did not know. A remand was necessary, for a further investigation of the books.—Ssperintendent Monaghan stated that he arrested prisoner, and in answer to the charge he said, "If I pay the amount now, can I go ?"—Mr. Steel, who defended, applied for bail, and said prisoner had an answer to the charge. He thought that when the Bench heard the full circum- stances they would agree that there was no case of em- bezzlement. Prisoner was a married man with two children, Several well-known Warrington men in sub- stantial positions were willing to go bail for him—Mr, Browne strongly opposed the application.—The Bench granted bail, prisoner in £lOO and two sureties of £lOO each.

YELLOW HAMMER,

MABEL BEAMES.

A certain old fairy tale tells us of " A youth who could neithn shiver nor shake." Such a youth was Gilbert Homo when he joined his re- giment, which had been quartered in India for litany years. The official name of the regiment I am not at liberty to disclose, neither is it of the least importance here. Suffice it to say that they were known throughout India as " The Khabardars !" Gilbert Homo joined them straight- from Sandhurst, where he had made both entrance and exit with honours : he was one of the new school and had ben " crammed " for exams, almost since he had been able to read and write. He was exceedingly youthful in ap- pearance, and though past nineteen might easily have been mistaken for seventeen. He was of middle height, and slightly built, with a smooth, fair young face and golden hair. The latter was the most remarkable thing about Lim—it was such beautiful hair ; so unusually golden, so glossy and well-groomed that it was the first thing one noticed on seeing young Gilbert Home. He had not joined half an hour before lie was dubbed with a name that was destined to cling to him for his entire service.

" That's a chubby child they've sent us !" re- marked Captain Slade, the Adjutant, when Gil- bert was out of ear-shot. " Did you ever see such a head in your life ?" " He's like some bird they have at home," re- plied another, Uniacke by name, screwing up his mouth as he tried to remember the bird's name. " I know !" lie exclaimed. " He's just like a Yellow Himmel., isn't he ?" and the name clung. Gilbert Horne was not received with much en- thusisain by his regiment for the officers were not men who had entered the service flushed with brilliant literary victory. They had all just con- trived to scrape in, and consequently. looked rather askance at this new arrival, who had done such great things at his exams. 9 Gilbert Home did not smoke, which was another disadvantage, judging front Mr. Uniacke's ex- pression when he offered the former a cigar on his arrival, and was answered, " Thanks, I don't smoke."

" Afraid of spoiling his teeth !" said Mr. tini• Nske, contemptuously, when referring to the oc- currence afterwards.

" I don't wonder at that. He's got a glorious set !" replied Cliptain Slade heartily, who suf- fered martyrdom periodically from toothache. "Do you know, I think you're making rather a mistake about this little subaltern of ours," he went on musingly. " Why do you all take it for granted that he's a molly-coddle ? He can't help looking like a girl, can he 2 And, upon my word, his clean little face does one good to look at after all the dirty niggers we've been staring at these ten years, to say nothing of your own phi/ Uniacke, which is getting blacker overy day with this beastly Indian sun, or poor old Hortimer's which is as yellow as a guinea from ima hem h."

SCIENCE NOTES AND

GLEANINGS.

STONE MADE FROM SAINDUSI

The preparation of wood-stone is of interest from its furnishing an einployment for sawdust. It is an agglomeration of wood sawdust and cal- cined magnesia reduced to powder, with thorough mingling accomplished by the humid method. The material is pounded and ground, and then compressed, at first slowly, with light pressure in a preparatory press, and then for eight consecutive hours in a machine capable of developing a pressure of 41b. The finished pro- ducts are then subjected to the action of the hydraulic press. They are incombustible, im- permeable to water, and susceptible of taking on a nolish, which allows of their use ill orna- mentation.

===== 0002601_19060106_art0182.txt | included=true | included =====
THE NAVAL TOP.

- - -- - Mr. Otto Schlick, the chief engineer of the Hamburg Lloyd Company, has, according to landependanre Beige, devised an appliance for steadying ships at sea, which is based on the action of a gyroscope. By the aid of this ap- paratus the .rolling and pitching of the vessel is said to be overcome, and the ship becomes much more steady. Experiments have been carried out at Hamburg with an old torpedo-boat. The gyroscope, which consisted of a disc of metal weighing 1,0361 b., was driven at the rate of 2,300 revolutions per minute by means of a steam turbine. Before turning on the steam a record was taken of the movements of the vessel, which had a total weight of sixty tons. As soon as the turbine was at work all the oscillatory movements ceased. Trials will now be carried out with a vessel of increased tonnage.

===== 0002601_19060106_art0183.txt | included=true | included =====
FALLING INTO THE SUN.

In an interesting paper on the Lunar Theory in Nature, Mr. P. H. Cowell, of Greenwich Observatory; states that during that part of his investigations dealing with eclipses he has been forced to the conclnsion that the earth's orbital Motion is being slowly accelerated on account of a sensible retarding effect of the ether. "It may, seem curious," observes MT. Cowell, " that this 'resistance should accelerate the earth's mo- tion, but that would be the effect. The total enerl.7 is diminished, and this implies that the planet falls towards the sun, and consequently revolves faster in its orbit." The theory of an inter-planetary resisting medium was first broached to explain the movement of Enclic's Comet, but was subsequently abandoned as un- tenable. Mr. Cowell's practical readoption of it is therefore noteworthy.

===== 0002601_19060106_art0184.txt | included=true | included =====
ATMOSPHERIC DISCOVERIES

French scienze Las recently thrown down a re- markable challenge to the conclusions of Profes- sor. Dewar, on the übject of liquefied air. M Georges Claude has for years devoted himself tr the problems suggested by the liquefaction of air, and in a lecture before the French Society of Civil Engineers, he has announced the solution of the greatest of them—that of producing oxy gen in practically unlin:itod volume. Proceed. Mg from the great starting point, M. Claude went on to the extraction from liquefied air of pure oxygen and of pure nitrogen. Then he found that, like alcohol, liquefied air could be rectified, and that in the process of evaporation, nitrogen escaped first and oxygen last, while in the process of liquefaction the order was re- versed, oxygen corning first and nitrcgen follow- ing, while Professor Dewar supposed these two elements to be given off simultaneously. M. Claude's discoveries will, it is claimed, pro- foundly modify the accepted laws of metallurgy, chemistry, agriculture, and illumination, and will also affect medicine and hygiene. The great agricultural value of his discovery lies in the easy production of aramoniacal salts.

===== 0002601_19060106_art0185.txt | included=true | included =====
EXPERIMENTS WITH, POTATOES,

The death of Earl Cathcart recalls some inte- resting experiments carried out at his sug- gestion, which aimed at the production of a more robust and disease-resisting potato. These experiments consisted in cross-fertilising the potato (AS'olanum tubtrostern) and certain wild species of tuber - bearing solanums. The potato of our fields is intolerant of damp, and its native haunts are the higher Andes, where there is very little rain. So among the potatoes chosen for cross-fertilisation was Solanum Magna, discovered by Darwin growing in damp places in the Chonos Archipelago. The work of rearing this potato was entrusted to a well- known English firm, and tubers of Solanurn Mag- lia and other wild potatoes were sent to them by Earl Cathcart. When the flowers appeared they were fertilised with pollen from the best disease-resisting varieties, and the Solanum Maglia yielded seeds. These were sown, and from them a new race of potatoes has been reared. Whether it will ever be put on the market as a practical success, and thus solve the problem of producing a disease-proof potato, or not, remains to be seen.

===== 0002601_19060106_art0186.txt | included=true | included =====
SIR WILLIAM RAMSAY ON RADIOBES.

Sir William Ramsay does not believe in the organic nature of Mr. Burke's radiobes. The radium bromide, he points out, which Mr. Burke sprinkled on the gelatine broth medium would give off the usual emanations. The grains would sink gradually into the gelatine, and the emana- tions would decompose the water into oxygen and hydrogen. Now the solution of these gase- ous products of radium in water have the power of coagulating albumen. Hence in a liquid con- taining this substance ultra-microscopic cells would doubtless be formed. The oxygen and. hydrogen are given off in atoms, or molecules, and surround themselves with sacks or bags of albuminous matter. It has been shewn, for ex- ample, that when solutions containing the game- OW emanations are injected under the skin of a living being the bubbles surround themselves with sacks or bags, the walls of which are only slowly absorbed by the organism. Mr. Burke's ratliobes are probably produced us follows: The gases from the decomposing water form minute bubbles, and the coagulative action of the emanations on the albumen surround, each with a skin. The product appears like a cell. and Mr. Burke calls it a rudione. The con- tenta of the cell are gaseous, a mixture of oxygen and hydrogen. The emanations of radium en- closed in each cell would go on decornpo:ing water, for water would diffuse through the cell walls. Then the cell wall would burst. gas carrying with it radium emanations would issue, and coagulate more albuminous matter. New MIA would thus be formed, and the old cell would seem to have multiplied itself. Thus the original bubble would resemble a yeast cell. and the others buds front it. And this budding would be repeated, Sir William Ramsay points out, as long as the radium gave out emanations, and the whole process would resemble the growth of a living being.

NOVEL ENGINEERING FEA2.

News comes from Toronto of the successful re- sult of the remarkable scheme of Mr. Isham Randolph, . the engineer of the Chicago Drainage Canal, to raise the water in the intake of the Niagara Falls City Waterworks, which had been gradually getting lower in consequence of the abstraction of water for the great electric power works above the' falls. For this purpose a lofty tower, about 70f1.. in height, had been con- structed by the river bank on the Canadian side, close to the place • selected for the dam. The lower part of the tower, for a height of 20ft.. was formed of timber trestle-Work or staging. and the 'ifest was of cement concrete 7ft. 4in. square in section, weighing about 200 tons. This massive erection, after the concrete, had, during some weeks' exposure, become - thoroughly in- durated. was tipped Over into the river by means of jacks, the operation taking about fifty minutes. As the tower lost its balance the third section from the bottom broke across, the con- crete having been intentionally divided into six by tha introductum woode!? wedges

extending partly across tne area. Ttie vast Mare (IF concrete was almost parallel with the surface of the water as it struck, and with a loud report great volumes of water and many large boulders were thrown into the air. When the air cleared, the dam was seen lying in the river in the position it was designed to occupy, but it was only partly it,lbmerged. The section next to the top struck a boulder in the :?iver bed and broke in two, but, owing to the presence of heavy chains embedded in the concrete, the broken _ _ _ _

ections were not much displaced. On the whole, the dam is pretty nearly where it was wanted. It he almost at right angles from the shore, j,ist, below the ice-rack of the intake, and is right in the way of a fast and deep current. A barrier reef, situated somewhat further out in the stream, throws the water between the dam and the intake. The substructure of 20ft of tim- ber was washed out as designed, leaving a race- ,ay some 15ft wide for the escape of ice along the shore., The result of these operations has onen to raise the level of the water by about 12in at the outside ice-rack, and at the head o! the waterworks flume there is 7in. more in depth 0f water, while in the well at the pumping- station there is an increase in depth of 10in. it was intended to raise the water at least a foot at i,take. so that the dam can only be regarded as having partiany aitamcu its oi...k:c. it remains to be seen whether during the period of very low water this unique engineering scheme will prove an entire SIMMS.

MANIFESTO OF THE WOMEN'S LIBERAL ASSOCIATION.

A Word for Husbands and Brothers.

I The Warrington Women's Liberal Association has issued the following manifesto to the electors : GENTLEMEN, We venture to address you on behalf of the women o the above Association in regard to the exercise of your votes in the coming election. Although many of uti rent and own houses pay local rates, and bear all the burdens of citizenship, we have no voice in electing representatives to Parliament. Being unal3l3 by law to exercise the rights which ought to accompany taxation, you will, we feel sure, readily admit that a 3 women we have much at stake in the great Free F•od Issue which is to be decided, as far as IVarrington is concerned, in a few days by your votes. / FREE TRADE AND HOME IMPORTS. IWhile our husbands, brothers and sons, have mainly to do with what economists call exports, in contributing ' their share of time and strength to the world's, work and receiving a money consideration for it,it is as a rule, , the women who have to spend this money in procuring the imports required for home use. Woman's work is to secure these imports in the form of food, clothing, &c., of the best value at the lowest price.. Hitherto we , have had all the world to buy from, we have had the j world's free produce and the world's oargains at our door, and our money has gone further and bought more than the money of any women iu the world. If you value an abundance of home comforts for your wives ; and children; then safe-guard your rights by voting for the candidate who stands for free food and untaxed necessaries. DIMINISHED PURCHASING POWBR RESULTS IN LESSENED, DEMAND FOR. LABOUR.

It seems clear to us that the effect of taxing food and other home necessaries must result in lessened, purchasing power. Where the income is limit. I, and will not buy so much, something mast be done without_ The industries manufacturing the article sacrinced must, as a consequence of the lessened demand, employ less labour, pay less wages, increase the namber of the I unemployed, tend to reduce wages, nn i bring m'sery to many homes. THE CHILDREN'S LIQUOR RILL. The evils resulting from intern pomace press very hard on the women and children of our land, and all reasonable attempts to lessen these evils ought to secure the approval of emery right-miuded man and woman. Whatever ho your views about the general question of the use of intoxicating liquors, all should desire, regardless of party, to safeguard our children from the associations which too frequently gather round the sale of intoxicating liquors. We notice with great pain that the present member fi.r Warrington' Mr. Robert Pierpoint, voted against the second rending;of the Sale of Intoxicating Liquors to Children Bill. Thisl3lll, brought in by the Conservative party, was only opposed by a few who appear to have sacrificed their independence of judgment and party ties for the purpose of supporting the brewer's monopoly. The second reading was carried by :374 and only 56. against, Mr. Pierpoint being one of the SG.

- 0 - THE HAPPIEST CHRISTMAS I HAVE

EVER SPENT. The happiest Christmas I ever spent was two Years ago. We had a concert when we broke up school tor our Christmas holidays and the snow was laying very deep on the ground. The cop- cert was held in the school-room the Thursday night before Christmas Day. and I took part in it. We had a month's holiday from school, so I had nothing to do except play with my sisters, and we had great fun at snowballing and. sliding. I went to a great many Christmas parties, and we also gave one at home. We invited a great many of our school-fellows, and in the afternoon we were playing games, and in the evening we had a Christmas tree, games and singing. I en- joyed myself very much on Christmas Eve and Christmas Day, and we had a great many Christ- mas singers that year. When 1 went to bed on the Christmas Eve I hung up my stocking at the foot of my bed, as many children do, ready for Santa Claus, who comes down the chimney at midnight with all kinds of toys, and oranges, apples, nuts, and cake and many other nice things. ALICE COATES.

Dear Uncle Ned,— I am sending you a story out of one of my books. It is called "Up a Volcano in Japan," and I think if it is good enough to print your nieces and nephews vill like it. I am glad you made me a member of the Children's League. I did not think my last story would be good enough for you as I made it up myself. I am very fond of reading. Ido hope you will like it. With love from your little CONNIE SAUNDERS. UP A VOLCANO IN JAPAN. A lady who has just returned from Japan gives the follow mg account of her ascent of an active volcano It had been a glorious August day, and we were all saying to each other, "What a night we Shall have for climbing Asimi Yama !" We had arranged to climb the volcano then, in order to see the sunrise the following day. The heat was too intense to do any climbing during the day, and the ascent was sufficiently easy to make it safe by the light of the full moon. We were staying at Karnizawa, about 18 miles distant from the volcano, and many a time during our visit we had rushed from our beds, our work, or our meals when the cry was raised " Asami Yama is at work!" and we had seen smoke 'pouring forth from the crater, sometimes in clouds, sometimes in a great pillar, generally white, but in the even- in&glorified by the glow of the setting sun. There had been no serious eruption for at least a century, but the soil of the surrounding country Must sometime have been scattered there by the volcano. For miles around the trees never seem to have a chance of growing to any size, though the fields were full of wheat, maize, and rice, and Country gay with lilies and campanulas. Thus on that bright summer evening a party of about 3o _people prepared for their midnight ent adv , ure. There were pack horses for the ladies and some of the gentlemen, led by little Japanese s_e_r,vants called " bettos" ; we had to trust our- Zolves to their tender mercies. What a funny to unt it Was! A horse with a saddle coming up 11 a Point back and front. On either side there clointiig giiipat.miers with supplies of food and extra There was no bridle, and I had to sit with my feet straight out in front of me, hanging ,!aver the horse's neck, and nothing to hold on to but the point of the saddle sticking between my knees. Certainly there were stirrups, but they were made of rough rope, and I was only allowed to putt my heels into them and not my toes—be- cause if I had fallen forward I should have dragged and not been able to free my feet. It was not a restful position. We had had enough of it by the time we were safe at home. * It was quite dark soon after we started. Our party rode in two divisions, some in front and some behind; but my betto seemed to object to company and went on steadily between the two. It was of no use for me to object, I only knew two Japanese words, and they weie not to the point. It was a horribly rough path ; I felt far from safe on my horse; the moon had not yet

"HO, FOR SLUMBERLAND " • A little song for bedtime, when, robed in gowns of white, All sleepy little children set sail across the night Foe that pleasant, pleasant country where the pretty dream-flowers blow, 'Twixt the sunset and the sunrise, "For the Slumber Islands, ho!" When the little ones get drowsy and heavy lids droop down To hide blue eyes and black eyes, grey eyes and eyes of brown, • A thousand boats for dreamland are waiting in a

row, And the ferrymen are calling, "For the Slumber Islands, ho ! " Then the sleepy little children fill the boats along the shore, And go sailing off to Dreamland; and the dipping . of the oar In the Sea of Sleep makes music that the children only know, When they hear the boatmen's "For the Slumber Islands, ho !" Oh! take a kiss, my darlings, ere you sail away from me In the boat of dreams that's waiting to bear you o'er the sea; Take a kiss and give one, and then away you go A-sailing into Dreamland. "For the Slumber Islands, ho!"

"THE MAN IN THE BOY."

_ Have you ever wondered what is meant by saying, " The boy is father of the man 7 ? Here 3re a -Jew stories which may help yen to see how 'rue it is. A Swedish boy tell out of a window, ..ind was badly hurt, but, with clenched teeth, he kept back the cry of pain. The king, who saw him fall, said that such a boy would make a use- ful man in the time of danger. And so he did, for he became a famous General. An old painter used to watch a little boy who amused himself making drawings on his paint box and his stool. The ,painter said, "That 134 will beat me, one day.' He did, for he became the famous painter and sculptor, Michael Angelo. Boys can all be- come great men if they care to do so, simply by attending to their habits. They must get rid of 411 bad habits, and try to learn good ones.

MY BIRD "DOT."

His name was "Dot," and he was the tiniest mite, not larger than a good-sized bumble-bee. I found him one morning last summer after a severe windstorm, lying helpless, with one of his gauzy wings injured in such a way that he could not use it for flying. He was not at all fright ened wken I approached and picked him up, but looked appealingly at me out of his very small black eyes. I could not but admire the elegance Of his dress, .showing green and gold with a glowing patch of red on his breast, while his feathers were perfumed with the scent of marg. flowers. •

Naturally, 4c) small a bird did not equire mansion to in. Indeed, *Dot" tried to tell me, in the way birds have of talking, that a cozy abode would meet with his approval. I found that a paste-board box would answer th@ purpose, and when I had strewn the bottom with sweet-smelling leaves, and put a twig across it, in the way of furniture, " Dot" was installed in his new home.

He would rest quietly on his perch, dreaming, as I imagined, of the days that were gone, of the blue sky, the sweet June breeze, until, re- collection proving too strong, he would try to use his wings. Then, alas! instead of bearing him up as they were wont to do, they could give him no support, but left him to fall to the floor of his house, there to lie patiently waiting for someone to replace him in an upright position. Every morning " Dot" and I made a tour of the garden, his specks of feet resting confidently on my enormous finer. We visited every blossom in turn, and he took a little honey from each. When the season- of flowers was over, I made a mixture of sugar and water to take the place of his natural food. He did not appear to distinguish any lack in the flavour of this make-believe honey; and when I let a drop of it form on the end of my finger, he was always ready to run out his long tongue (which looked like a thread of silver) and si pit off. He seemed to thrive on this artificial diet, and would no doubt be living now had I not one fatal day placed the dish containing it too near him. 1 left him musing in his quiet way over past de- lights, but returned to find his body floating, on this sticky sea, with his dear little feathers in sad disarray. Poor " Dot "! His trials Were over, and I consoled myself by fancying that he was away in the humming-birds' heaven, happy in a garden of flowers, of which we have never seen the like.

LONG -VERSUS SHORT ENG ,GI'3I.I.I,NTS

BY TIM

LADY A-VA. CAMPBELL.

It is impossible to lay down a hard and fast rule on this subject. Poor humanity now-a- days is so seldom found capable 'of the strain and endurance, which a protracted matrimonial [engagement entails. l'erhaps we do Illar, inherit 'the patience of our grmidparents. Whnt m;ay the proverbs, the epitomised wisdom of human experience : " Marry in haste and repent at leisure." " Happy's the wooing that's not long a-doing." " Time tryeth Troth." As I write these words, memory recalls from the far dis- tance a beautiful picture. of married life. A stately couple over sixty, the wife especially handsome, as tall as her military husband, their mutual love and confidence only equalled by the boundless pride and affection they showed for their family of twit) daughters just grown up. These wedded lovers ' were engaged twenty years. They illustrate the old song— " E'en those who smiled to see us part Shall see us meet with wonder, Such trials only make the heart • That truly loves, grow fonder. Our sorrows past And' he our pride When, with each other vying Thou wilt confide in hint who lives Upon thy truth relying." •

Another couple tell a different story. He as, an ruidergraduate engaged himself to his "dove- eyed young cousin "; and for ten years they waited. He was a clergymen, and at last aliv- Mg was offered less than 1.300. a year. On this they married. They had not seen very lunch of each other in the long interval, nor had they felt consuming anxiety to hasten the event. It all proved very disappointing. 'The wife often described in after years the 'misery of her home- coming. No preparation bait been made for her. lack of paint, and torn papr and general dis- comfort struck her painfully. tihie cried herself to sleep in her new home, and wondered bow he " could have brought her to such a place." title has been crying more or less ever since, end has developed a temper which goes far to destroy any chance of harmony in the home. ts*.he was affectionate to a degree, capable of any amount of devotion, but his indifference to her comfort chilled her better feelings, and she never en- tirely forgave him. If they had only seen niorn of each other before marriage, she would have been disillusioned, and they would have Ip:tied by mutual consent, or would have slarti'll on matrimony's troubled sea with a clearer under- standing of each other's character.

Tn F. ENGAGEMENTS Or OUR GRAMMAR ENT s.

The happiest woman I know married after a very short engagement, and she told Int- many were the forebodings of evil to mile, Iseause matters Were so easily and so quickly 111.1aitged. She followed the fashion of her family, an illus- trious one; her grandparents met at a ball, mar.- ried soon, and as in old romances lived happily ever after ; her parents were equally precipitate and fortunate, and as fcr herself up to now the gods have showered every good gift upon her, beautiful, talented, well-born, mated in her own degree, she and her splendid children are a picture of happiness rare to see. Judging from old stories our grandparents were far more im- pulsive than ourselves. One story used to de- light me. " Aunt, aunt," said a flushed. breathless, excited girl at the end of an even- ing's entertainment, "he has asked me, and I have said yes." " Calm yourself, Louisa," re- plied her dignified relative in measured tones, but there was no calm in the matter. A gallant young fellow eagerly pressed for an interview next morning, and in a short tune two young hearts were betrothed, and married with parem- tal consent, and though they never met till that night, had no cause to regret the hasty decision. Another rather absurd story, 1 can vouch for, and years ago the old couple were pointed out to me. A regiment of militia were bi•ing marched Into a country town. As they crossed over ii bridge, it tall girl attracted the captain's atten- tion ; his eyes followed her with ardent gaze, and he involuntarily uttered.his thoughts aloud, " That is my wife if ever I have one." It all shortly came to pass, and I remember the old lady telling my mother " they had been mar- ried more than forty years, and would gladly have every year of the time over again." Sadden, emotions are by no means Always to be trusted ; yet ii good deal of experience con- firms my belief in the truth of first' impressions. 1 have often for a while changed the opinion had formed at first sight of a person's elitsstc- ter, and almost always after still furtblr ac- quaintance, reverted to my original impreseott. And so I am a believer in love mid friendship ,t first sight. Some of us are quick to recognis.,,,, kindred sp nt. and in a higher degree, but also ln common with the animal creation, we poscess instincts which seldom betray us.

SORE INSTANCES—TRUE LOVE AND pßrEnnsm.... One might multiply instances on both sides. One - happy couple 1 know were engaged three weeks. They had met a few months previously. Another equally happy. He made up his mind in the first ten minutes of their acquaint- ance that if possible he would win her, and their engagement was proportionately short, followed by four years of happiness—ended by death. Another fell in love aii. boy •of twenty, and for twelve long years carried her photograph abont in his wanderings, and her image in his heart. She knew nothing about it, and had many lovers. At last they met, were engaged three months, and married, but they are not mated. rather manacled together. The beauty and charm which took his young heart captive and which some women seem never entirely to Jose are still powerful, but she has other gifts which he cannot appreciate, and in aim of life, in mind and heart, they are as far asunder as the poles. I wish to write only of true love, very rre perhaps, but " this love is of the heart, the mind, the brain," and to such lovers a long ,m- -gagement, when it entails sepanttion, especially, means a great deal of suffering. " the dull deep anguish of patience." :Much depends, however, on the tempennuent. There is another side to all this. " Absence bestows a refined and, aerial delicacy upon affection, which it with difficulty acquires any .other way. It seems to resemble the communication of spirits without the Medi- um or impediments of this earthly frame." lint surely this can be true only of those rarefied and exalted mantes one meets but seldom, but re- members always, 'A clever writer on this subject remarked lately, I am told, in a leading ladies' paper that a Jong etaga,enient was harassing front this point of view A woman does not like to feel that her lover is among the hindmost in the nice of life; others are successfi il. but not he, failure attends his well directed efforts. He has ability and industry primps, but lacks that power of seizing the opportunity, striking while the iron is Init, that goes .so far to ensure success. As she sits on, eating her heart out, with some hard natures, love, or what she took for love, degenerates into an almost con- temptuous pity. COMPANIONSUTP, AND VSIVERSAL NERD. Now trAy should engagements be rang Parents are often to blame, they forget their own youth. they minimise the importance of pl'irrillge. To quote Dean Pijon, " Marriage is Oh I' second to the crdinance of the Sabbath." It is the list, rill state of manhood and woman- hood. The celibate life on either side is un- natural. It is it sacrifice to lose the son or daughter who contributes all the brightness left to life. Let them erown the dignity of parent- age, which in itself involves so much self-de- nial with_ this further renunciation.

But a far more prevalent reason is love of ma- terial good. We must hare so much luxury or comfort. We cannot marry as our parents ' did. I have a theory that there is only so much power in each of ns, and if we spend too freely on the gratification of one part of our being, the other goes starving. Companionship, after re— ligion, is the highest need of our nature. In- deed why religion so answers to our great,' wants is because it reveals a Presence, whose ' love and sympathy never leave us desolate. Now human love is the reflex. of this. No eitse' of life, no fullness of purse can be compared to , the " love of men and women when they love their best, closest and sweetest." Does it satisfy " to walk in silk attire and siller tine to spare ? when we have to sit down by the ' fireside with one whose ideals and principles and tastes differ radically from our own ? how lonely life is, where there is no real friendship, no answering sympathy in our hour of need. lu what lie the riches of life? THE ELECTION CAMPAIGN,

POLLING DAY FIXED,

Warrington's Wave of Liberal Enthusiasm.

REPORTS OF THE WEEK'S MEETINGS.

In a little over a week the electors of Warrington will be called upon to return a representative to Parlia- ment. The• writ for the election is expected to be received on Tuesday next, and on that assumption the Mayor has definitely fixed the polling day for the following Monday—January 15th. Nominations must be in by January 12th. With the near approach of the momentous day, both parties are hard at work and meetings have already been held in several wards. On the Liberal side there is every confidence as to the issue and remarkable enthusiasm and unity prevail amongst the army of workers who have rallied in defence of Free Trade and all the best that Liberalism stands for. Of mural literature there is already a great display, the hoard- ings having been literally "painted red"with a sprink- ling of blue. Next week there will be meetings every night and electors of every part of the town will have the issues effectively placed before them.

did not want to draw in Mr. Crosfleld, but if he was in the House of Commons ko would go so far as to give Home Rule to Ireland for all domestic uses but he would never go so far as to give a Parliament to Ireland independent of this country. However much they would like to give perfect

HOMZ RULE to Ireland he would not allow anything which would in the sljghtest degree endanger or bring about a separa- tion of Ireland from England, but he wanted to see Ireland treated fairly and in a manner which was con- sistent with good government and common sense. (Ap- plause.) With reference to the fiscal policy he hoped he took a broad minded view of the question. He was always being told that German competition would be the undoing of this country and that we should never, ' under any circumstances,take any goods from Germany. He was in the unfortunate position of sending goods to Germany and it was possible that if we kept out the German goods his German friends might object to receive his gooda. (Hear, hear.) He had travelled in Germany and made the acquaintance of German work- ing men and if there was one thing whicn the German envied more than any other it was the position which the English workingmen occupied. If he wrote to-morrow and told the German workingmen that he could find work for them in England they would tumble over each other to get here and to leave their own protected country. Germany sent to us £39,000,000 worth of goods while we sent to Germany goods to the value of £36,000,000 so that there was no very great difference. There was,however,adifference and that was that while we sent to Germany manufactured in partly manufactured goods, Germany's export, in a large measure consisted of raw materials and food stuffs. (Applause.) He had visited Saxony the centre of the German textile industry and bad compared the con- ditions of work with the conditions in Lancashire. The German commenced work at 6 o'clock on Monday morning and finished at 6 o'dlock on Saturday night and while in Lancashire the average wage was 255. per week the German received 15s. or 183. In Saxony beef,' as we knew it,was very much dearer than in Englandand the men could not afford to buy it and had to eat horse meat. He did not see anything wrong in eating horse I meat but he fancied that if a round of horse meat were offeted to a British workingman there would very soon be a row. (Applause.) If the German could not get horse meat he eat dog meat and he knew it to be a positive fact as he bad spoken to men who had clubbed together and bought a dog and then divided it. A German newspaper stated that the horse slaughter-houses in Munich were becoming too small. Ten years ago 500 horses were killed where as last year the number had increased to 3,000. Last month 375 horses were killed in Munich, the largest number ever known, and the paper went on to say "This number it is expected will be exceeded in December as more than 100 animals were killed during the first week of the month." Germany was highly protected and be believed we would have the same condition of things in England if Protection was adopted. (Applause.) The imports into England of food and drink and raw materials amounted to £413.000,000 while only £135,000,000 worth of manufactured articles were imported. Their Tory friends stated that the unemploy- ment in England was increasing. He had taken out figures with reference to that. The only trades in which there had been a falling of in employment had been the textile industry, dressmaking and tailor- ing. To compensate for this there had been

AN INCREASE r THE NUMBED. EMPLOYED in transport of 24 per cent., in building 36 per cent., in metal workers 26 per cent., coal mines 24 per cent., paper and printing 26 per cent., and furniture and wood 27 per cont. (Applause). He had lived in the days of Protection, and had seen potatoes 50s. a load. He had known working men who only tasted butcher's meat once a year. He would be told that in that he was exasgerating, but he could assure them that it was a positive fact. (Hear, hear.) lie had seen men working in the leather trade at Latchford, when he was an apprentice, young, strong, and intelligent men, working from 6 o'clock in the morning until 6 o'clock at night, for six days in the week, for 2s. a day. (Applause.) Under Free Trade he had seen the hours of work decrease in number, and wages go up, and if he might advise ' them he would say" by all the power which you have I stick to the big loaf which Free Trade has brought into existence." (Applause). Mrs. HOLMES at this stage read the manifesto issued to the electors by the Warrington Women's Liberal Association, a copy of which we reprint in another COluran.

Mr. C. E. PARKER said he was downright proud of the Warrington women and was glad that the Liberal candidate recognized the justice of the claim which the women householders made for the right to vote. Women by paying taxes helped to keep the country and to his mind they had every right to help to govern the country. (Applause.) There was no doubt that Pro- tection was the great issue upon which the election would be fought although, as they would have noticed, the Tories were running away from the question and were seeking cover under the cry of retali- ation and imperialism. There was no doubt in his mind that if by any mischance a Tory Government was re- turned to power it would not be a question of retalia- tion but a question of Protection. (Applause.) Mr. Parker in conchtsion spoke of the conditions of living in Germany, stating that thousands of horses were ex- ported from England to that country where they were made into sausages which commanded a higher price than beef sausages in England. He Ms') clearly illu- strated how the introduction of Protection would divert energy to trades which were least profitable and take away energy from trades which were moat profit- able. (Applause.) Alderman Pierpoint in calling upon Mr. Crosfleld said their candidate would only address them briefly as he Nr4i reserving his remarks for Friday's demonstration.

PROGRAMME OF MEETINGS.

The frllowing is the programme of meetings:— This (Friday) evening, Parr Hall, chairman, Mr. C. E. Parker. .

Monday evening, Thick-street Mission, chairman, Councillor Magian. Tuesday evening, Orford School, chairman, Coun- cillor Wilkinson. ' _

Wednesday evening, National Schools, chairman Councillor Clegg. Thursday evening, St. Peter's School, chairman, Mr. W. Furness. _ _

_ Friday evening, St. James' School, chairman, Alderman Tinnion. Fairfield School, chairman, Mr. George Walker. Saturday evening, Parr Hall, chairman, Mr. C. E. Parker. _

Addresses will be delivered at these meetings by the candidate (Mr. Crosfield), Mr. Wm. Madden and others. In addition to the above meetings, the Junior Liberal Association has arranged for a meeting in the Workingmen's Mission School, Thewlis-street, on Wednesday evening, at which the candidate Will speak. On Friday evening, a meeting under the auspices of the Progressive Labour League will he held in Sankey Bridges Wesleyan school. Mr. A. Birch will preside, and the speakers will include Mr. .T. Elwell and Mr. John Walters (ironworkers), and Mr. Thomas Barnes.

ENTHUSIASTIC PROCEEDINGS.

THE GREAT ISSUE.

The Liberal election campaign opened most encouragingly on lyeduesday evenintz with a public meeting iu the Parish Room,- Lateliford. The pro- ceedings nu.gurecl well for the success of the peat cause, for their WaS a crowded_ attendance and the

greatest enthusiasm prevailed. Not a single dissentient note was raised and% resolution pledging support to Mr. A. H. Crosfield was carried unanimously. Alderman Pierpoint presided and was supported on the platform by the candidate, Mr. W. Madden, Mr. C. E. Parker, the Rev. Joseph Ellison, Mr. E. J. Guest, Mr. and Mrs. C. Jackson Holmes, Mr. and Mrs. Felix Maginn, Mr. Joseph Howes, Mr. Shakeshaft, Mr. Bryant and others.

ALDERMAN PIERPOINT AND PROTECTION. Alderman Pimom said that hail he consulted his ease he would not have occupied the position which he had assumed that evening, but he thought , the importance of the occasion was such that it would not be right even for the oldest member of the Liberal party to refuse to put his hand to the fight that Was before them. In all his political experience, which extended over a great number of years he did not know of any political fight which had happened in War- rington which had been of greater importance than the one in which they were eagaged and which he hoped would be carried on strenuously, honestly and with fair play. (Hear, hear, and applause.) He was one of those broad-minded politicians who recognized that it was a good thing to have a strong Government but he claimed that it was also good that there should be a strong and honest opposition in order that they might criticise and amend if desirable any question of political importance which might be put before the country by the party which was in power. He might criticise any intenaed action which might be regarded as honest by the party which proposed it, but which he looked upon as dis- honest and not in the best interests of the country,but he would do that cautiously and fairly and would not use personalities of any kind. (Applause). It had been said by one of his old friends that in a speech which he made a alien time ago at a meeting of thd Junior Liberal Association he used words which had a tendency to lower the standard of political fighting in Warrington. In his whole experience it had been his endeavour in Warrington to raise the tone of political fighting and to avoid personalities. (Hear, hear.) He desired publicly to assure his old friend that if he said anything which was in the direction.indicated be did not wish his remarks to behr such meaning and he tendered his apology. (Hear, hear.) They were at the start of a great political fight and he was pleased that the first meeting should be held in Latchford Ward and that there should be such a large audience. (Applause.) :We had had ten years' of undiluted Tory rule.-ten years in which the Tories had had a magnificent working majority and in which they night have done an incalculable amount of good to the country—but he ventured to say,that the great object of the past Governu.ent had been to maintain itself in office and to preserve the interests of .their friends. (Hear, hear). He would ask any workingman or any business man to point out if they could, any good to them personally which had resulted from the ten years' of Tory rule. (Applause). The Tory Govern- ment very cleverly passed I THE AGRICULTURAL RELIFF AUT.

which operated in the direction at lessening the rates paid by farmers by one half. That was all very well so far as it went but the taxes were placed on the shoulders of householders like himself. In other words in order that the agricultural party might pay less rates. The Tory Government made men, situated as he was, pay more. (Applause.) The Tory Government had also increased the expenditure of the country to a remark- able extent, and he, personally, failed to see where he had received the slightest benefit from that increased expenditure, and he did not see how workingmen were any better in consequence. (Applause.) He was once looked upon as a pro--13oer—(applause)—because he did snot believe in the sinister influence which were at work in order to bring about that war. (Applause.) He said they had that night had some slight taste of Mr. Crosfield's fighting qualities and they could judge by the manner in which he had reserved hirqelf that he was thinking a great deal of the responsibility which was placed upon his shoulders. They were members of a party which consisted of all the intellects as compared with the Tory party, because they were not men who blindly followed leaders. They were men,each in his individual capacity, who thought for themselves. They did not follow the lead either of Mr. Disraeli, Mr. Chamber- lain, or Mr. Balfour, and come up like beaten curs at the crack of the party whip. They took time to think for themselves and they stood on a platform like that. each with a full sense of the responsibility that rested upon hint, representing different nationalities anl shades of religion to which they belonged. PRINCIPLES OF _SOUND IMPERALISM

They were for freedom of mankind. They were banded together, Protestant and Catholic, Irishmen and Eng- lishmen, to struggle for one common cause. They wanted no bastard imperialism. They wanted the brotherhood of man throughout the world. They would carry on the fight to the end. The Digine fire of liberty flickered upon the altar of freedom and whenever it showed anysigns of going out it would be gently wafted into being light and strength by the wings of the angel of patriotism which was ever hovering over it. All those attacks of the masses and classes were vain things to pursue against the majeity of the people. In as much as they were on the ramparts of freedom they defied the attacks on the citadel. As in the past so to day they carried the flag of liberty on high and whenever an attack was made they would hurl them back with that grandeur of their race and character and with the tradi- tions which belonged to them as a party. (Applause.) ' Mr. JOSEPH" HOWES briefly seconded the resolution. Alluding to the foreign labour canard, he said the Liberals did not accuse Mr. Pierpoint of employing foreign labour. Their candidate was interested in Warring- ton and it would be" a Warrington man for Warring- ton" this time. (Applause.) The resolution was carried unanimolslv.

"PUERILE PERSONALITIES" DEPRECATED.

Mr. CROSFIELD thanked the audience most heartily for the cordiality with which they had received the resolution. (A Voice : " You deserve it.") He did not profess to have done much, but he was very proud, to whatever small extent he might be able to follow in the footsteps of predecessors in his family. Those who knew his father would indeed be sur- prised that anyone should try and insinuate that he had anything but the friendliest and the 'warmest feeling of affection for the town in which he was born and with which he had been associated all his life. Ho (Mr. Crosfleld) confessed to complete surprise that that insinuation ambngst others should have been thrown out. All of them on the Liberal side deprecated most strongly any introduction of those silly and puerilo personalities, but as regarded himself personally he could assure them that after twenty-two years in the soap trade and after having had lots of hard knocks in commerce, to him anything their friends chose to say on the other side was just like so much water on a cluck's back. He did not wish their opponents any ill, but it was a pity they did not spend their time to better advantage and he thought it was an evidence of weakness in thfsir cause. He believed that meeting and its enthusiasm were a good evidence of strength and a happy omen for the future of their cause. (Applause). CHAIRMAN THANKED. In moving a vote of thanks to the Chairman, Mr. CROSFIELD said that Alderman Pierpoint had since 13n3 been an active and powerful supporter of the Liberal party. They deeply appreciated the fact that there was not an atom of flagging in his energy, and that he was taking a very vigorous part in the present election. which was perhaps the most important they had over had in Warrington. (Applause.)

Enthusiastic Meeting at Winwick Street.

CHAIRMAN'S SPEECEL Mr. G 1: EST said that the Tory party had always had a bias towards legislation for the classes rather than the masses. The party was distinctly composed of aristocratic and wealthy people, who did not represent the democracy but rather the upper 10,000. (Haar, hear). The Liberal Government in the past had ever stood for retrenchment and reform, ancl if they would trust Sir He iry Campbell Bannertnan withthe government of the country he would do it better and more economically than the late government. Taken man for man and com- paring the present with the past government they were forced to the conclusion that the present government was greater in ability aril altogether superior to the late government. (Applause.) In Warrington they had to decide between Mr. Pierpoint and Mr. Crosfleld. While Mr. Pierpoiut never went beneath the surface, in Mr. Crosfleld they had a man who had done a good deal of digging, who had gone beneath the surface and had given lahorious year's of consider ttion to those questions, and who was in full sympathy with the people. They would endeavour to conduct themselves in such a way that whether they won or lost they would have nothing to be ashamed of. (Applause.) The Tories said the Prime Minister'saction with regard to stopping the importation of Chinese labour into Soiith Africa, was prompted by the basest of ruotivee, that it was a Rolitical trick, and that it was done be i'39 the election was panding.iThey asked if he was earnest why he did not, send hack the !47,090 Chinese who were in the Transvaal? The answer to the question was however perfectly obvious. They were not sent back because the Government could not do it as the Tory Government had allowed the Chinese to be indentured for a number of years, and until the indentures expired the Chinaman was the slave of his master. (Applause). When the sluestion was before the House of Commons Mr. Pierpoint voted for the Chinamen and every vote given to Mr. Pierpoint was a vote given for Chinese labour in South Africa.' (Applause). On all labour questions during the past two years Mr. Pierpoint had voted against the interests of the workers. Tan oresent fight was a struggle for the emancipation of Warrington and if they worked hard until the votes ware counted the victory would be theirs. (Applause). TtIE CANDIDATE'S SPEECEI.

Mr. A. H. CROSFIELD, who had a splendid reception, said he felt it a great responsibility to undertake the position which he owed to their kindness and favour, and to:the influence of the leaders of their party in War- rington. (Applause). (Applause.) His rpttlific itions were very limited and he should be a fraud if he pre- tended to tell them that ha had been able to cover the whole field of politics. IGNORANT OF THE CONDITIONS

----- under which they vens going to work, and the latest message received from Lord S3lborne confirmedthat. In the East gold mining was very much a. question of washing alluvial deposits and the China- man, to begin with, did not know that he was going to work underground. Another fact which every worker in Warrington ought to consider was that the mandarins of China had deported and were rooting out of their country and shipping into our country—because, of course, it was under the British flag—large quantities of the criminal class. Than they knew that there was physical punishment used in the mines. There was flogging ; a certain amount of starvation ; and other

, BARBAREC PRACTICES introduced from China. Well, they had been stopped as far as the mineowners and their managers or sub- managers were concerned, but at this present moment they had the State in the Transvaal, which we had created, by its agent inflicting that corporal punishment on the Chinese and therefore acting as slave-driver in British t writory. (" Shame.") As for the Chinese themselves, his hearers knew what it meant to them to live under conditions like that. There was no impetus to honest work—none at all eKcept what they got from the lash of the driver. There was no competition amongst employers for good workers, so that the mine owners might have a proper inducement to treat their employees well. if they did not call that slavery, he at least said they could call it nothing less than

A SPECIES OP SLAVERY. (Applause.) There were, continued Mr. Crosfield, two main causes to fight for although there were many others, but the two issues in the foreground were Chinese labour and Free Trade. (Applause.) He would like to quote them some figures which Mr. Torn Garnett, the very able secretary of the Free Trade League, a Conservative, a man of broad mind and great energy on commercial matters, gave to him. They concerned the export trade of Lancashire cotton and he would not apologise for quoting them although he had submitted them to a kVarrington audience before. He asked Mr. Gsrnett h)w far Lancashire cotton spinners were affected by German competition in the great neutral markets of the East, and he was told that the export of Lancashire cotton to India in round figures was about £20,000.000 worth a year whereas Germany , only sent about £200,000 worth. (Applause.) He (Mr. Crosfield) happened to know A gentleman who had charge of the chief cotton mills of Providence, in America, and a very able man he was too. S 3 he asked Mr. Garnett how he found the Americans troubled thorn in India,and the reply was that about £200,000 would cover the exports to India froth the Providence mills. (Applause.) \Vas not that a marvellous indication of the INDIENSG ADVANTAGE

which Free Traders held in India audio. all the neutral markets? (Applause.) That was recognized as far as the neutral markets proper were concerned. But what he thought wa.s sometimes overlooked was that when we'were competing in a country which was protected by big tariff barriers, that country was still a neutral market to England. If we were competing say in America with another Protectionist country, wa had the advantage of cheap production that enabled us to beat our competitors. The export per head of the population from Britain to Protected countries was therefore much greaterthan it was from other countries. It was, in fact, nearly twice as much per head as from any other country in the world. (Applause.) The industry with which he was associated—a much smaller one than the cotton trade but one which concerned the working people of Warrington to some extent—practi- cally commanded the expoq soap trade of the world. lie did not say that England got it all, but they found that three firms on the Kersey by reason of cheap pro- ' (Notion were able to beat any kind of foreign competi- tion. (Applause.) Not only were they able to beat Germany—the most progressive people—in the Eastern and in all the neutral markets, but they were able in spite of the resources which the United States had I in respect of raw materials, to beat their American corn-: petitors at their own doors in the West Indies—(loud nplause)—and where freights were perhaps'22s.a toe for English and only 10s. a ton from New Orleans and other celitres in America. Though the Americans fought them fora few years, they had had to knuckle under at the finish. because owing to the expense of living and labour combined, which was about 2.1 i times as great as it was in England, they could not compete with the English firms in the cost of manufecture. (Applause). After the Liberals had defended Free Trade, and finally put a stopper on this preposterous agitation, there was

NIECII WORK TO BE DONE, which he hoped and trusted, if they would give him their support, be might be able to discuss there and perhaps. in some small degree, be able to help to further. (Applause). He was thankful that they were going to fight that battle right through with clean hands. They might be sure that he was not going to shirk the paltry questions which their opponents had raised in the hops o° making a little political. capital. He was no saint— he was a man of the world—but he was not ashamed of the relations he had had, with labour for the last twenty two years. (Applause). He intended on Friday evening , to give an answer to these paltry personal questions, and he ventured to think that the answer would be just a little more than their Tory friends had bargained for. FOREIGN LABOUR CANARD. TORY CHALLENGE ACCEPTED.

An interesting stag 3 has been reached in the con- troversey initiated by the Conservative party as to the number of foreigners employed by Mr. A. H. Crosfteld. In the hope of making capital the Tories appealed to the electors not to vote " for the man who employs u large, number of foreigners." This insinuation was immediately refuted by Mr. H. Roberts, a managing director of ffessrs. Joseph Crofield and Sons, Ltd., who stated that the number of foreigners employed by his firm was 3 out of 2,000 workpeople. The poster in which this statement was set forth was headed " How the Tories tell the truth." The next day the following poster occupied a large portion of space on local bill- posting boArdings : "The Tories never tell lies or resort to dirty practices. Thes3 are Radical methods.
