Protest Meeting' of Crosfields'

Work 111 aa.

A crawded raeeting yaw hold of the employes of nessrs. Croslelds' in the Assembly Room of their works, on Tuesday evenin;. Die meeting was called by the employes to protest against the m Inner in which thefll.l,ol3 of the firm and its policy had been recantly attacked. _ _

Mr.T. A. WILKISSON was elected to the chair and said : Fellow-workmen, this meeting hie been called to give our opinion of the poster that has been* put on the walls with regard to foreign employment at these works. Well, I think there is not a single person em- ployed by the firm who does net know that the substance of these placards is false and who does not resent the I slur that has been cast amen the firm. As we all know the foreigners within these works could almost be counted on the fingers of one hand. They are nearly all connected with the technical staff, all therefore highly necessary to the firm's operations and consequently indispensable if the firm is to contiaue as in the past to give us employment. I have much pleasure in call- ing upon Mr. Long to put a resolution. Mr. LONG then propesel " This meeting of the employes of Joseph Crostield and Sons, Ltd., repre- senting both political parties, desires to record its indignation at the absolutely false aspersions which have been cast upon the policy and management of the firm and the recent attempts to bring its mama into odium by references to foreign labour." Mr. DA.LE, in secendiug this resolution, said: I think that everyone of you will agree no matter a. het political opinion you mayihold.that the placard referred to should not have been placed upon the walls of this town. (Hear, hear.) Not only does the poster e nbody an ignorant fallacy but it is in the highest degree mis- leading. Let us assume that a stranger visits Warring- ton for the first time a ram unaceeainted with Cros- field's world. What impression will it make oa his mind when he reads that placard? Will he not at once conclude that a vast nurneer of foreigners are employed at these works? Now we want fair play, we are not holding this meeting for political and partizan purposes. (Hear, hear.) All we ask is that every employee whatever be his shade of politics shall come here to register his opinion of these tactics, and this we shall do simply because we belong to the firm. We are proud of it, and we moan to be loyal to it Now, ladies and gentlemen, we know perfectly well that these placards are intended to reflect not merely on Mr. Arthur Crosfielrl, but more especially on our Managing Director, Dr. Markel, who has been responsible for the introduction of the foreign gentlemen referred to on the placard. You know as well as Ido that Dr. Markel has introduced these gentlemen from a purely business point of view. They are not workman as you and I understand it from the placard. They are specialists brought here to introduce business which could never otherwise have found its way into Warrlngton—(hear, hear)—and their introduction has beee the means of enlarging the wage-earning community of this town, and surely no one can complain of that. (aaar, hear.) I hope, therefore, that regardless of ow political and party feeling we shall all be in accord in expressing our opinion of these proceedings. You are not asked to give your vote in favour of one candidate or the other ; as to that, you all kaew the abaolutely fair position the firm takes up and the perfect liberty it allows to its employds, but I ask you, my fellow ernployds, to express your indignation at this scandalous proceeding with no uncertain voice. (Applause.) Mr. T. A. Moses, in supporting the resolution said : As this is an indignation meeting I suppose I ought to be indignant. Well, gentlemen, f feel too sorry ant have no room for any other feeline, but sorrow. •T air a Conservative ; I will go further, I am a red-hot Tory, A few days ago when I gave Mr. Arthur Croseeld ray reasons for not giving him my votehe !Aid : etiaroa Morse, I must say you hit out straight from the shoulder." (Applause.) But to-day for the first time in ray life I feel ashamed of my party, or I should say a small section of my party. That Raster the Conserva- tives got out is hietine below the belt ; it is alio, and the very worst kind of lie that can be spoken, namely, a lie founded on truth. I would like to mention that as there are so many Conservatives in this firm,some time ago a resolution was passed mat one of the man ager'e meetings to the effect that politics were not be brought inside the works, and this resolution was afterwards confirmed by the directors. surely this sneaks for itself. (Applause). Now let us quietly consider this foreign labourquestion. Let us begin at the most ,recent events and work backwards. There is a, process entirely, or almost entirely, unknowa in England. We import one man to teach fie how to work this proesssr and what is the result ? The V.B. department, number 1, 2 and 3 refineries, and what is the effect ? Those processes have given employment to, shall I Sey. 103 men ? And again there is the erection of the different plants which have given employment to many more Warrington men with wives and families. Now again there are four or aye men foreigners, who are solely engaged in research work. Is thet teking the bread out of anyone's mouth in Warrington ? No ! But this will in time give work to many hundred more hands. lam going to speak about our friend Dr. Markel. This is the man the Conservatives are hitting at. I ask you who is the man ha this firm who his brought process after process to perfection, giving work to hundreds of men? When I came here a few years ago we employed about 500 hands and now it is over 2,000, and who has done it? Dr. Markel. Do you know that Dr. Markel's mother before she was married was a Miss Caroline Cooper, of Cavisham, Berkshire, of a good old English family ? And this is the man whom the ConFerva fives in their ignorance are hitting below the belt. (Great applause.) The motion was then put from the chair and, no amendment being forthcoming, was carried nem. con., with enthusiastic applause. • Mr. TIIONIAS RIGBY then proposed " This meeting, recognizing with extreme regret the painful position in which the incident in question has placed certain persons, ns seizes with pleasure this opportunity of emphasieing the happy personal relations of the employde with the management, and not least with Dr. Markel and the foreign members ofAhe staff." He also appeared before them as a convinced Conservative. His only object in proposing the resolution was to secure fair play—fair play for Mr. Crosfield, and fair play for the firm, to which they were all attached. Mr. WALKER in briefly seconding the resolution said that for himself the last 10 years of his experience of Dr. Markel, and perhaps he might say everybody's eeperience of the doctor during that time, had proved Dr. Markel's usefulness, not merely to the firm, but to the town and district of Warrington. THE ECONOMICS OF FREE TRADE. --- Meeting of the Junior Liberal Association.

A meetinz of the Warrington Junior Ll'oet,l Asgocia- don was held on Friday evening at Gregory's co 4, at which Mr. C. E. Parker delivered an address on The economics of Free Trade." Mr. Hdruund, (president). presided over an excellent attendance. Mr. H. Gray read letters whL-ih h h 4 recoicEd from Mr. A. If. Crnsfield, Mr. C. E. Parker, Mr. Roberts, Mr. A. Rtthbone, Ir J. G. Withinshaw. Mr. E. J. Guest, Alderman Tinuion, Rev. C. Harvey Cook, who wrote accepting the ocaoes of vice-presidents. Mr. A. H. Crostield wrote : I think we all feel how much the future political history of Warrington will be affected by the issues of the present struggle, personally I am sanguine of the result, but whether the issue may be, it is a fine thing to feel we have such a splendid cause—or rather.l should say, such splendid causes to light for as a genuine progressive force in the politics of the nation and the world." In his letter Mr. E. J. Guest said : " Your Associatioa is one of the most hopeful signs that Liberalism in War- rington presents. I trust that the vigorous signs of life manifested by your Association will be maintained and increased as the days go by. If the young men grow in knowledge of the past, and in the needs of the present the prospect for the future is bright. Warrington has been held so long by the Tory party as the result of ignorance and Indifference."—Mr. A. Rathbone expressed the hope that the Association would succeed in awaken- ing that interest, which had been so lamentably lacking among the youn; men in past and current politics.

_

Mr. OSBORNE said he had received a further letter from Mr. Croatiald in which he said : "I am delighted that every man is enthusiastic to the finger tips. Never in our time has there been so great a cause—or indeed so many great i causes to rally us to the standard of progress. There are many years of work before thei party of progress, because we have not only to eraseaf as far as possible the consequences of mischievous aad , reactionary government during the last decade but at: ithe same time, and afterwards to strive for yet higher. 1 and nobler ideals of civilization in the future. We can. , dearly recognize all this without in the least losing our ' sense of proportion, or failing to perceive that our first duty is to defend that freedom of trade which 1 has been such a godsend and blessing to old England: ' When those who seek to cover up disastrous blunders revive this old cry of Protection we may simply point out to them that "An individual does not grow, rich by increasing his or her cost of living and expenditure. Neither does a nation." Protection notoriously spreads corruption amongst the governing classes and civil servants, and prevents progress by causing lethargy and lack of enterprise amongst empleyers, as well as envy and disunion amongst the working-classes themselves. Why then should we be so foolish as puts premium on political and commerical corruption and re-action just to make the nation as a whole poorer? The onus of proof lies with those who make this preposterous pro- posal ! but they give us no proof because they cannot." (Applause) Mr. C. E. Parker, who had a hearty reception 4 at the outset dealt with the question of exchange between nations illustrating clearly that the more a country got in and the loss it sent out the better it was for that country. England, he said, was in. that advantageous position because of her Free Trade policy. Trade naturally gravitated in the direction of that country which offered the best economic conditions and the best economic conditions could not be found under Protection. A System of giviurr' certain trades a preference, other trades would be handicapped and. there would be a distinct economic loss. (Hear, hear.) Comparing the social status in Geralanv with that an this country Mr. Parker pointed out that in 1901 27,313 were sent from England to Germany the average value of eaoh animal being £9 Four thousand were less than £3. Aged People's Treat. On Thursday in the Parr Hall, Warrington,. 47 men, all aged over sixty, were entertained to dinner. This annual treat is provided by public subscription. The fare consisted of roast beef, pork, mutton, vege• tables, and plum pudding. with beer and tobacco. In the evening tea was provided for 700 aged women. It is to add to the --- luxury and comfort of this temple of domestic virtue that he cheerfully foregoes matrimony, EADE'S GOUT & RHEUMATIC PILLS. sometimes for many a year. In this I think he errs, but it is a noble error. To my mind. the ll_ less tables and chairs, and pictures and gilding and bric-a-brac, the more simple the life the better. Probably another reason for long engagements nowadays is the fear of failure in married life. There are those who would fain hover on the brink, they dread the finality of matrimony, and that also is the reason of many worldly marriages. Will love last 2is the cry of such. If we marry to obtain material good, it is there, but this love so beautiful, will it endure ? Am I not giwping at the shadow, and losing the sub- stance which would give ease and enjoyment to life ? Again I nmst say, much depends on tem- perament, something on cirmunstanro. Many are perfectly incapable of appreciating the highest good. It is nise for each individual to recognise the needs of his own nature, and plan life accordingly. " Everything has its price and the price buys that and not something else," and those who marry for love will probably have much to sacrifice in the way of ambition and material good. To 811111 up. No engagement, generally speak- ing, except after a long previous acquaintance, should be shorter than three months, nor if pos- sible longer than three years.
